<!-- defn-1: body_md -->
\citep{dwork2006our}
    A randomized mechanism $\mathcal{M}$ is said to satisfy $(\varepsilon,\delta)$-DP if for all pairs of neighboring datasets $(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')$ differing by one record, denoted by  $d(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')=1$, and for all subsets $\mathcal{S}\subseteq$ image$(\mathcal{M})$, it holds that
    \begin{equation}\label{eqn:DP}
    \Pr(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{Y})\in \mathcal{S}) \leq e^{\varepsilon} \Pr(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{Y}')\in \mathcal{S})+\delta.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end defn-1 -->
<!-- defn-2: body_md -->
\citep{bun2016concentrated, sommer2018privacy}\label{def:loss}
For a randomized mechanism $\mathcal{M}$, let $o\in \text{image}(\mathcal{M})$ be any potential output of $\mathcal{M}$ and let $\mathbf{X}_0, \mathbf{X}_1 \in \mathcal{X}$ be two inputs. The privacy loss random variable $\mathcal{L}$ associated with releasing $o$ is defined as
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:PrivLoss}
    \mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{X}_0)\|\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{X}_1)}(o)\!=\log\!\left(\Pr(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{X}_0) = o)/\Pr(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{X}_1) = o)\right).
\end{equation}
<!-- end defn-2 -->
<!-- defn-3: body_md -->
\citep{mironov2017renyi}\label{def:rdp}
A randomized mechanism $\mathcal{M}$ is said to have $(\alpha, \varepsilon)$-RDP if for all pairs of neighboring datasets $\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'$, it holds that
\begin{equation}
    D_{\alpha}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{Y}) \|\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{Y}'))\le \varepsilon,
\end{equation}
where $D_{\alpha}(P\|Q)$ is the R\'enyi divergence \citep{renyi1961measures} of order $\alpha > 1$ between distributions $P, Q$:
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:renyi_div}
    D_{\alpha}(P\|Q) =(\alpha-1)^{-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{x\sim Q}\left(P/Q\right)^\alpha = (\alpha-1)^{-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{x\sim P}\left(P/Q\right)^{\alpha-1}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end defn-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:DPconversion}
Let $\mathcal{M}$ be a randomized mechanism, and $\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'$ be any pair of neighboring datasets satisfying $d(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}') = 1$. Then,
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin =*, itemsep=0pt]
    \item $\mathcal{M}$ is of $\varepsilon$-DP if and only if $D_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{Y})\|\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{Y}'))\le \varepsilon$ \citep{mironov2017renyi}\footnote{$D_{\infty}(P\|Q) =\sup_{x\in\text{support }P}\log(P/Q)$ is the max divergence between $P$ and $Q$ when $\alpha=\infty$.}.
    \item $\mathcal{M}$ is of $\rho$-zCDP if and only if $\;\forall \alpha \!\in\! (1, \infty)$, $D_{\alpha}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{Y}) \|\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{Y}'))\!\le\!\rho\alpha$ \citep{bun2016concentrated}.
    \item If $\mathcal{M}$ is of $(\alpha, \varepsilon)$-RDP, it is also of $(\varepsilon\!+(\alpha\!-\!1)^{-1}\log 1/\delta, \delta)$-DP for $\forall \;0 < \!\delta\! < 1$ \citep{mironov2017renyi}.
    \item If $\mathcal{M}$ is of $\rho$-zCDP, it is also of
    $\sqrt{2\rho}$-GDP. If $\mathcal{M}$ is of $\mu$-GDP,
    it is also of $\mu^2/2$-zCDP \citep{dong2022gaussian}.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lemma:c2_in_c1}
     $c_2 = \log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})}\left[\exp(-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta))\right].$
     Specifically, if there exist finite constants $a, b \in \mathbb R$ with $a<b$ such that $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta) \in [a, b]$ for $\forall \boldsymbol\theta\in\boldsymbol\Theta$, then $c_2\in [-b, -a]$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:D_alpha_general}
Let $P=\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})$ and $Q=\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')$ be a pair of neighboring posteriors. With $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ and $c_2$ defined  in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:decomp}, the R\'enyi divergence of order $\alpha>1$ between $P$ and $Q$ is 
{
\begin{align}
    D_{\alpha}(P\|Q) &=c_2 +(\alpha-1)^{-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\label{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2}\\
    &= \log\Big[\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim P}\left[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\right)\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha-1}}\Big] \text{ per Lemma~\ref{lemma:c2_in_c1}}.\label{eqn:D_alpha_general}
\end{align}}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:reverse}
Theorem~\ref{thm:D_alpha_general} holds for any ordered pair of neighboring posteriors. Swapping the roles of $\mathbf{Y}$ and $\mathbf{Y}'$, let $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\triangleq\log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y}')}{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})}=-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$; then
\begin{align}
D_\alpha(Q\|P)=\log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim Q}\big[e^{-\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\big]+\tfrac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim Q}\big[e^{(\alpha-1)\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\big],\label{eqn:D_alpha_reverse}
\end{align}
with expectations taken under $Q=\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')$. Since $d(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')=1 \Leftrightarrow d(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})=1$, $(\alpha, \varepsilon)$-RDP requires $\sup_{(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'):d(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')=1}\max\{D_\alpha(P\|Q),D_\alpha(Q\|P)\}\le\varepsilon$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- coro-1: body_md -->
\label{coro:uniform}
Suppose that $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ is uniformly bounded, that is, there exist finite constants $a, b \in \mathbb R$ with $a<b$ such that $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta) \in [a, b]$ for all $\boldsymbol\theta\in\boldsymbol\Theta$. 
Define $\Delta \triangleq  b-a$ and $A \triangleq e^{-\Delta}\in (0,1), B \triangleq  e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}>1$. 
Then, for every $\alpha>1$,

\@ixpt\@xpt

  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@  \@plus 2\p@

  \belowdisplayshortskip 3\p@ \@plus 2\p@   \@minus 2\p@
\@ixpt\@xpt

  \belowdisplayshortskip 3\p@ \@plus 2\p@   \@minus 2\p@

\begin{align}
\max\{D_{\alpha}(P||Q),D_{\alpha}(Q||P)\} \le \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta) \triangleq \log\left(\frac{(\alpha-1)(B-A)}{\alpha(B-1)}\right)
+\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\!\left(\frac{B-A}{\alpha(1-A)}\right),\label{eqn:D_alpha_ub}
\end{align}}
and releasing OPS satisfies $(\alpha, \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta))$-RDP.
<!-- end coro-1 -->
<!-- coro-2: body_md -->
\label{coro:pureDP}
Under the conditions in Corollary~\ref{coro:uniform}, for every $\alpha>1$,
{
\begin{align}
\max\{D_{\alpha}(P\|Q),D_{\alpha}(Q\|P)\}\le b-a = \Delta = \sup_{\alpha>1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha,\Delta),
\end{align}}
implying OPS satisfies pure $\varepsilon$-DP with $\varepsilon = \Delta$.
<!-- end coro-2 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{eg:categorical}
    Let $Y \!=\! \{y_i\}_{i=1}^n$ be a dataset, where each $y_i\!\in\! \{1, \ldots, K\}$ is a categorical outcome. Suppose $y_i|\boldsymbol\theta\overset{\text{\fontsize{6pt}{7pt}\selectfont i.i.d}}{\sim}\text{Categorical}(\boldsymbol\theta)$, where $\boldsymbol\theta\!=\!(\theta_1,\dots,\theta_K)$ with $\sum_{j=1}^K \theta_j \!=\! 1$ and $\theta_j\!=\!\Pr(y_i\!=\!j)$. Let $s_j(Y)\!=\! \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{1}\{y_i=j\}$ denote the $j$-th category count.\vspace{-3pt}
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*), leftmargin = *, itemsep=-3pt]
    \item Bounded neighboring ($Y, Y'$): there exist distinct indices $j\neq k$ for $j, k \in \{1, \ldots, K\}$ such that
    $s_j(Y)=s_j(Y')+1,
    s_k(Y)=s_k(Y')-1$, and $s_\ell(Y)=s_\ell(Y')$ for all $\ell\notin\{j,k\}$.

    \begin{align}
    &c_{1, \mathrm{bounded}}(\boldsymbol\theta) = \log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};Y)}{\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};Y')} = \sum_{\ell=1}^K \big(s_\ell(Y)-s_\ell(Y')\big)\log\theta_\ell = \log\frac{\theta_j}{\theta_k},\notag\\[-3pt]
    &D_{\alpha, \mathrm{bounded}}\big(P\|Q\big) \le \max_{j\ne k}\Big\{\!\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\!\left[\theta_k/\theta_j\right]+
    (\alpha\!-\!1)^{-1}
    \log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\left[\left(\theta_j/\theta_k\right)^{\alpha-1}\right]\!\Big\}.\label{eqn:cate_bounded}
    \end{align}}
    \item Unbounded neighboring ($Y, Y'$): 
    there exists a unique index $j\in \{1, \ldots, K\}$ such that
    $s_j(Y)=s_j(Y')+1$, and $s_\ell(Y)=s_\ell(Y')$ for all $\ell\ne j$.

    \begin{align}
        &c_{1, \mathrm{unbounded}}(\boldsymbol\theta) = \log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};Y)}{\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};Y')} = \sum_{\ell=1}^K \big(s_\ell(Y)-s_\ell(Y')\big)\log\theta_\ell = \log\theta_j,\notag\\[-3pt]
        &D_{\alpha,\mathrm{unbounded}}(P\|Q)\le\max_{j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}}\Big\{\log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\big[\theta_j^{-1}\big]+(\alpha-1)^{-1}\log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\big[\theta_j^{\alpha-1}\big]\Big\}.\label{eqn:cate_unbounded}
    \end{align}}
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- coro-3: body_md -->
\label{coro:subgaussian}
Suppose that there exists a variance proxy $\sigma_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2\in(0,\infty)$ such that for $\forall t\in\mathbb R$, it holds that $\log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}(e^{t(c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)-\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})})\le t^2\sigma_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2/2$
where $\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}\!=\!\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\left[c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\right]$.  Define
\begin{align}\label{eqn:scale}
\textstyle \bar{\sigma}^{2} \triangleq \sup_{(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'): d(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')=1}\sigma_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2,
\end{align}
where the supremum is over all ordered neighboring pairs.
Then, for every $\alpha>1$,
\begin{align}\label{eqn:eps_subG}
\max\{D_{\alpha}(P||Q), D_{\alpha}(Q||P)\}  \le  \varepsilon_{\mathrm{subGaus}}(\alpha, \bar{\sigma}^2)\triangleq\alpha\bar{\sigma}^2/2,
\end{align}
and releasing OPS satisfies $(\alpha, \varepsilon_{\mathrm{subGaus}}(\alpha, \bar{\sigma}^2))$-RDP.
<!-- end coro-3 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
When $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ is sub-Gaussian, the inherent DP guarantees of OPS are conceptually equivalent to applying the Gaussian mechanism with scale parameter $\bar{\sigma}^{-2}$ defined in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:scale}.
\vspace{1.5pt}
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:GLM}
Suppose there exists a finite constant $L>0$ such that for every \((\mathbf{x}_i,y_i)\) and every \(\boldsymbol\beta\), $\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta}\log p(y_i| \mathbf{x}_i,\boldsymbol\beta)\right\|\le L$, and impose a Gaussian prior on $\boldsymbol\beta$, i.e. $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta)\!=\! \mathcal N(0,\tau^2 \mathbf{I}_d)$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*), leftmargin = *, itemsep=-3pt]
    \item Bounded neighboring $(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')$: $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is sub-Gaussian under $ \pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$ with variance proxy  $\sigma_{\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'}^2\le 4L^2\tau^2$ for every ordered neighboring pair and, per Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian}, 
    \begin{align}
    \max\{D_{\alpha}(P\|Q),D_{\alpha}(Q\|P)\}  \le \varepsilon_{\mathrm{GLM, bounded}}(\alpha) \triangleq \alpha\bar{\sigma}^2/2 \le 2\alpha L^2\tau^2.\label{eqn:eps_GLM_bounded}
    \end{align}
    \item Unbounded neighboring $(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')$: $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is sub-Gaussian under $ \pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$ with variance proxy $\sigma_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2\le L^2\tau^2$ for every ordered neighboring pair and, per Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian}, 
    \begin{align}
    \max\{D_{\alpha}(P\|Q),D_{\alpha}(Q\|P)\}  \le\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GLM, unbounded}}(\alpha)\triangleq\alpha\bar{\sigma}^2/2\le\alpha L^2\tau^2/2.\label{eqn:eps_GLM_unbounded}
    \end{align}
\end{enumerate}
Eqs.~\eqref{eqn:eps_GLM_bounded} and \eqref{eqn:eps_GLM_unbounded} taken together, releasing OPS satisfies $(\alpha, 2\alpha L^2\tau^2)$-RDP.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{eg:logistic}
Logistic regression is a special case of the GLM above with binary response $y_i\in\{0,1\}$, log-partition function $\psi(t) = \log(1+e^t)$ and $h(\cdot) = 0$. Suppose \(\|\mathbf{x}_i\|\le R<\infty\) for \(\forall \;i\), then the condition in Proposition~\ref{thm:GLM} holds with $L=R$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*), leftmargin = *,itemsep=-3pt]
    \item Bounded neighboring $(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')$: WLOG, assume ($\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'$) differ in the $n$-th observation. 
    \begin{align}
    \max\{D_{\alpha}(P\|Q),D_{\alpha}(Q\|P)\}&\le\varepsilon_{\mathrm{logistic, bounded}}(\alpha)\notag\\
    &=
    \begin{cases}
        \alpha R^2\tau^2/2 &\text{ if }\mathbf{x}_n=\mathbf{x}'_n (\mbox{and thus } y_n\ne y'_n) \\
        2\alpha R^2\tau^2 &\text{ if }\mathbf{x}_n\ne\mathbf{x}'_n (y_n\ne y'_n \mbox{ or } y_n= y'_n) \\
    \end{cases}\label{eqn:eps_logi_bounded}.
    \end{align}
    \item Unbounded neighboring $(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')$: Letting $L=R$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_GLM_unbounded} yields 
    \begin{align}
    \max\{D_{\alpha}(P\|Q),D_{\alpha}(Q\|P)\}\le\varepsilon_{\mathrm{logistic, unbounded}}(\alpha)= \alpha R^2\tau^2/2\label{eqn:eps_logi_unbounded}.
    \end{align}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:GG}
    Let $\mathbf{Y} \!=\! \{y_i\}_{i=1}^n$ be the dataset, where $y_i|\theta \overset{\text{\fontsize{6pt}{7pt}\selectfont i.i.d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\theta, \sigma^2)$ with known variance $\sigma^2$. With Gaussian prior on $\theta$: $\pi(\theta)= \mathcal N(\mu_0,\tau^2)$, 
    the posterior is $\theta|Y \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_Y, v_Y^2)$, where $v^2_Y = \sigma^2/\omega$ and $\mu_Y=\mu_0 + \sum_{i=1}^n (y_i-\mu_0)/\omega$, with $\omega = n + \sigma^2/\tau^2$.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*), leftmargin = *, itemsep=-3pt]
    \item Bounded neighboring $(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')$: WLOG, assume $\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'$  differ in their $n$-th record $y_n$ and $y'_n$. Then $c_1(\theta)$ is Gaussian with variance $\sigma^2_{Y, Y'} = (y_n-y'_n)^2/(\sigma^2\omega)$. If all records lie in an interval of length $2k\sigma$ for some $k>0$, then $\sigma^2_{Y, Y'} \le \bar{\sigma}^2 = 4k^2/\omega$, and per Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian}\vspace{-3pt}
    \begin{align}
    \max\{D_\alpha(P\|Q),D_\alpha(Q\|P)\}\le\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GG, bounded}}(\alpha,\omega) \triangleq \alpha\bar{\sigma}^2/2= 2k^2\alpha/\omega.\label{eqn:eps_GG_bounded}
    \end{align}
    \item  Unbounded neighboring $(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')$: WLOG, assume $\mathbf{Y}'$ is obtained by removing the $n$-th record $y_n$ in $\mathbf{Y}$. Then $c_1(\theta)$ is not sub-Gaussian; nevertheless, each term in the exact expression of the R\'enyi divergence $D_{\alpha}(P\|Q)$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general} can be analytically derived using the moment-generating functions (MGFs) of the non-central $\chi^2$ distribution with one degree of freedom and non-centrality parameter $(\mu_Y\!-\!y_n)^2/v^2_Y$. Specifically,

    \begin{align}
        D_{\alpha}(P\|Q) 
        & = A(\alpha, \omega) + \frac{(\mu_Y\!-\!y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2} B(\alpha, \omega)\label{eqn:DPQ_GG_unbounded} \\
        &A(\alpha, \omega)=-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1\!-\! \omega^{-1}\right)-\frac{\log\left(1\!+\!\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega}\right)}{2(\alpha-1)}, \quad B(\alpha, \omega) = \left[\frac{1}{1-\omega^{-1}}-\frac{1}{1\!+\!\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega}}\right],\notag\\
        D_{\alpha}(Q\|P)
        &=
        \begin{cases}
            \widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)+\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2}\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega) &\text{if } 1<\alpha<\omega,\\
            \infty & \text{if } \alpha\ge\omega,
        \end{cases}\label{eqn:DQP_GG_unbounded}\\
        &\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)=-\frac12\log\frac{\omega}{\omega-1}-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\frac{\omega-\alpha}{\omega-1},\quad \widetilde B(\alpha,\omega)=\frac{\alpha\omega}{(\omega-1)(\omega-\alpha)}.\notag
    \end{align}}
    If for all $i$, $|y_i\!-\!\mu_Y|\le k^*\sigma$ for some $k^*>0$, then for $1<\alpha<\omega$,

<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{eg:LR}
Let each data record be $(\mathbf{x}_i,y_i)$, where $\mathbf{x}_i\!\in\!\mathbb R^d$ is the covariate vector,  $y_i\!\in\! \mathbb{R}$ is the response, and $y_i|\mathbf{x}_i,\boldsymbol\beta \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta,\sigma^2)$ with known variance $\sigma^2$. Let \(\mathbf{Y}\!=\! \{(\mathbf{x}_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n\) be the dataset, $\mathbf{x} \in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$ be the design matrix, $\mathbf{y}\in \mathbb{R}^n$ be the response vector. With Gaussian prior $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta)\!=\! \mathcal N(0,\tau^2 \mathbf{I}_d)$, the posterior $\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y} \!\sim\! \mathcal N(\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y},\Sigma_\mathbf{Y})$, where $\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}^{-1}\!=\!\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}\!+\!\tau^{-2}\mathbf{I}_d$ and $\boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathbf{Y} \!=\! \Sigma_\mathbf{Y} \left( \sigma^{-2} \mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{y} \right)$. Assume $\|\mathbf{x}_i\|\le R<\infty$ for all $i$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*), leftmargin = *, itemsep=-3pt]
    \item Bounded neighboring $(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')$ with $\mathbf{x}_n \!=\! \mathbf{x}'_n$: WLOG, suppose the $n$-th record $(\mathbf{x}_n,y_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}$ is replaced by $(\mathbf{x}_n,y'_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}'$, where $y_n\ne y'_n$. Then $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Gaussian under $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$ with variance $\sigma_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2=(y_n-y_n')^2\mathbf{x}_n^\top\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{x}_n/\sigma^4$. If \(|y_n-y_n'|\le 2k\sigma\) for some \(k>0\), then \(\sigma_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2\le\bar\sigma^2=4k^2\tau^2R^2/\sigma^2\), and per Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian}
    \begin{align}
    \max\{D_\alpha(P\|Q),D_\alpha(Q\|P)\}\le\varepsilon_{\mathrm{LR, bounded}}(\alpha) \triangleq \alpha\bar{\sigma}^2/2= 2\alpha k^2\tau^2 R^2/\sigma^2.\label{eqn:eps_LR_bounded}
    \end{align}
    \item Unbounded neighboring $(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')$: WLOG, suppose $\mathbf{Y}'$ is obtained by removing the $n$-th record $(\mathbf{x}_n,y_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}$. $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is not sub-Gaussian under $ \pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$. Define $\omega_n =\sigma^2/(\mathbf{x}_n^\top \Sigma_\mathbf{Y} \mathbf{x}_n)$. With $A,B,\widetilde A,\widetilde B$ defined in Proposition~\ref{thm:GG},
    {

    \begin{align}

        D_{\alpha}(Q\|P) &=
        \begin{cases}
            \widetilde A(\alpha,\omega_n)+\frac{(\mathbf{x}_n^\top \boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}-y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2}\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega_n), &\text{if } 1<\alpha<\omega_n,\\
            \infty, & \text{if } \alpha\ge\omega_n.
        \end{cases}\label{eqn:DQP_LR_unbounded}
    \end{align}}
    Since $\omega_n\ge\omega_{\min}\triangleq1+\sigma^2/(\tau^2R^2)$ for every admissible pair, if for all $i$, $|\mathbf{x}_i^\top \boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y} - y_i|\le k^*\sigma$ for some $k^*>0$, then for $1<\alpha<\omega_{\min}$,

    \end{align}}
\end{enumerate}

<!-- coro-4: body_md -->
\label{coro:subexponential}
Suppose that there exist $\nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2\in(0,\infty)$ and $b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}\in(0,\infty)$ such that for $\forall\;t\in\mathbb{R}$ satisfying $|t|<b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^{-1}$, it holds that $\log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\!\left(e^{t(c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)-\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})}\right)\le \frac{t^2\nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}|t|)}$
where $\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\left[c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\right]$. Define
\begin{align}\label{eqn:eps_subexp}
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{subExp}}(\alpha, b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}, \nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2) \triangleq \frac{\nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2}{2}\cdot
    \frac{\alpha(1-2b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})+2b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})\bigl(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}(\alpha-1)\bigr)}.
\end{align}
When $b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}<\min \{(\alpha-1)^{-1}, 1\}$ for all ordered neighboring pairs, 
{
\begin{align}
\max\{D_{\alpha}(P||Q), D_{\alpha}(Q||P)\}  \le \sup_{(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'): d(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')=1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{subExp}}(\alpha, b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}, \nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2),
\end{align}}
and releasing OPS satisfies $(\alpha,\sup_{(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{subExp}}(\alpha,b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'},\nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}))$-RDP.
<!-- end coro-4 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:LR_case2}
When $\mathbf{x}_n \neq \mathbf{x}_n'$, $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ contains both affine 
and quadratic terms in $\boldsymbol\beta$ and is generally not 
sub-Gaussian under $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$. Suppose $\|\mathbf{x}_i\|\le R < \infty$ and $|y_i| \leq M_y< \infty$ for $\forall \;i$, and assume the scale 
restriction $\sigma^2 > 4\tau^2R^2$, then $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is sub-exponential with parameters
{
\begin{align}\label{eqn:para_LR_ne}
b_{\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'} = \frac{4\tau^2R^2}{\sigma^2}, \quad 
\nu^2_{\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'} \leq \frac{8M_y^2R^2\tau^2}{\sigma^4} + 
\frac{16\tau^4R^4}{\sigma^4} + 
\frac{8n^2\tau^6M_y^2R^6}{\sigma^8},
\end{align}}
which can be plugged into Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential} for explicit RDP bound.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The posterior of parameter $\boldsymbol\theta$ given data $\mathbf{Y}$ is
\begin{align}\label{eqn:BayesRule}
    \pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}) &= \frac{\pi(\boldsymbol\theta)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mathbf{y}_i|\boldsymbol\theta)}{\int \prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mathbf{y}_i|\boldsymbol\theta)\pi(\boldsymbol\theta) d\boldsymbol\theta}
    \overset{\triangle}{=}\frac{\pi(\boldsymbol\theta)\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})}{\int \ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})\pi(\boldsymbol\theta) d\boldsymbol\theta}                                                                                              
    = \frac{\pi(\boldsymbol\theta)\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})}{p(\mathbf{Y})}.
\end{align}
                                                                                                         
For any pair of neighboring datasets $\mathbf{Y}$ and $\mathbf{Y}'$ where $d(\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}')=1$, the privacy loss for releasing posterior sample $c(\boldsymbol\theta)$ per Eq~\eqref{eqn:PrivLoss} can be decomposed as:
\begin{align}
    c(\boldsymbol\theta)
    &=
    \log\left(\frac{\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})}{\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')}\right)=\underbrace{\log\left(\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})}{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y}')}\right)}_{\text{

  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip

  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@

  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
 $\triangleq\; c_2$}}. \label{eqn:decomp_ape}
\end{align}
Note that $p(\mathbf{Y}')$
\begin{align}
=\;&\!\!\int\! \ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y}')\pi(\boldsymbol\theta) d\boldsymbol\theta  = \!\!\int\!\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y}')}{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})}\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})\pi(\boldsymbol\theta) d\boldsymbol\theta =\!\! \int\!\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y}')}{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})}\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})p(\mathbf{Y}) d\boldsymbol\theta\;\;\mbox{ per Eq.~\eqref {eqn:BayesRule}} \notag\\
=\;&p(\mathbf{Y})  \int\!\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y}')}{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})}\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})d\boldsymbol\theta\notag\\
\Rightarrow \;\;&\frac{p(\mathbf{Y}')}{p(\mathbf{Y})} 
=\int\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y}')}{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})}\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}) d\boldsymbol\theta = \int e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})d\boldsymbol\theta = \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right].
\end{align}
Since $c_2 = \log(p(\mathbf{Y}')/p(\mathbf{Y}))$,  we have
\begin{equation}
c_2 
= \log \left(\frac{p(\mathbf{Y}')}{p(\mathbf{Y})}\right) = \log\left(\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\right). \label{eqn:c2_in_c1}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Per the definition of R\'enyi divergence in Eq~\eqref{eqn:renyi_div},
\begin{align}
    D_{\alpha}(P || Q) =\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{x\sim Q}\left(\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}\right)^\alpha
    = \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{x\sim P}\left(\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}\right)^{\alpha-1}.
\end{align}
For any neighboring datasets $\mathbf{Y}$ and $\mathbf{Y}'$, we can calculate the R\'enyi divergence between the corresponding neighboring posterior distributions $\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})$ and $\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')$ as below
\begin{align}
    D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})\|\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')) &= \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[\left(\frac{\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})}{\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right]\notag\\
    &=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)c(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\notag\\
    &=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1) (c_1(\boldsymbol\theta) + c_2)}\right]\quad\text{ per Eq~\eqref{eqn:decomp_ape}}\notag\\
    &=c_2 +\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1) c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\label{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}
\end{align}
Plug Eq.~\eqref{eqn:c2_in_c1} into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}, 
\begin{align}
    D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})\|\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}'))=& \log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]+\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\label{eqn:D_alpha_general_ape}\\
    = & \log\Bigg[\!\left(\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\right)\left(\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)\cdot c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha-1}}\Bigg].
\end{align}
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Assume $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ is uniformly bounded, i.e. there exist finite constants $a, b \in \mathbb R$ with $a<b$ such that $\forall \boldsymbol\theta\in\boldsymbol\Theta, c_1(\boldsymbol\theta) \in [a, b]$. Thus $-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\in[-b,-a]$ and per monotonicity. 
\begin{align}
c_2= \log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]&\in [-b, -a]\\
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1) c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right] &\in [a, b].
\end{align}
Taken together with the decomposition in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}, we obtain 
\begin{align}
    D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})||\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')) &=c_2 +\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)\cdot c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\le b-a.\label{eqn:b-a}
\end{align}
Note that to attain the \textbf{equality} in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:b-a} of the upper bound  $b-a$ would require simultaneously $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta) = a$ (so that $c_2 = -a$) and $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta) = b$ (so that $\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1) c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right] = b$) almost surely, which is impossible unless $a=b$. Therefore, this bound is not tight and unattainable.


                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 

To obtain an attainable upper bound of $D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})||\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}'))$, it suffices to maximize the two exponential moments of $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ involved in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:b-a}, subject to the constraint $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\in [a, b]$. This maximization can be achieved using convex-order arguments, as in \citep{shaked2007stochastic}. 
                      
                                                                                                    
                                                                                         
                                                                                                           
                                                                                                             
                                                                                                                                                                         
WLOG, let $X$ be any random variable uniformly bounded with $[a,b]$, and $X^*$ denote a binary random variable supported on $\{a,b\}$ as below, with the same mean as $X$:
\begin{align}\label{eqn:X*}
X^* =
\begin{cases}
a, & \text{with probability } 1-p\\
b, & \text{with probability } p
\end{cases};
\end{align}
that is, $\mathbb{E}(X)=\mathbb{E}(X^*)=a(1-p)+bp$ for some $p\in [0,1]$.
For any convex function $\phi$ and any $x\in [a,b]$, convexity implies the chord bound: 
\begin{align}
    \phi(x)\ \le\ \lambda(x)\phi(b)+(1-\lambda(x))\phi(a) \quad \forall x \in [a,b]
\end{align}
Let $\lambda(x)=\frac{x-a}{b-a}$ and take expectation of $\phi(x)$ over its distribution, we have:
\begin{align}
    \mathbb E[\phi(X)] \le p\phi(b)+ (1-p)\phi(a)=\mathbb E[\phi(X^*)].
\end{align}

\begin{lemma}[Theorem 3.A.7 in \cite{shaked2007stochastic}]
    Let $X$ and $Y$ be two random variables with the same finite means. Then $X \le_{\mathrm{cx}} Y$ if and only if,
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}(\phi(X))\le \mathbb{E}(\phi(Y)) \qquad \text{for any convex function $\phi$}.
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
Hence, per the Lemma above, we have $X \le_{\mathrm{cx}} X^*$. Since $X$ can be any random variable uniformly bounded with $[a,b]$, $X^*$ is maximal in convex order among all such
bounded random variables. 

Also, since the exponential function $e^x$ is convex, and the R\'enyi divergence functional $D_\alpha$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general} is fully determined by the exponential moments of $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$, it follows that $D_\alpha$ is monotone with respect to the convex order. Setting $X = c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$, we therefore obtain
$c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\le_{\mathrm{cx}} X^*$,  which implies that the $D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})||\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}'))$ is maximized when $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ coincides with the endpoint-supported binary random variable $X^*$ as defined in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:X*}. Then per Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_ape}
\begin{align}
    &D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})||\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}'))\notag\\
    =\;& \log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]+\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\notag\\
    \le\;&  \log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-X^*}\right]+\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)X^*}\right]\quad \text{per convex order dominance}\notag\\
    =\;&  \log\left((1-p)e^{-a}+pe^{-b}\right)
    + \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\!\left((1-p)e^{(\alpha-1)a}+pe^{(\alpha-1)b}\right)\notag\\
    =\;&\log\!\left(1-p+pe^{-(b-a)}\right)
    + \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\!\left(1-p+pe^{(\alpha-1)(b-a)}\right)\label{eqn:D_alpha_p}\\
    \triangleq\;& D_{\alpha}(p)
\end{align}
For a fixed pair of neighboring datasets $\mathbf{Y}$ and $\mathbf{Y}'$, in order to align the means of $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ and $X^*$, one should require,
\begin{align}
    &\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\big[c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\big] = a(1-p)+bp\notag\\
    \quad\Rightarrow\;&  \hat{p} = \frac{\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\big[c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\big]-a}{b-a},
\end{align}
However, since \(\mathbb E_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim\pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}[c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)]\) may take any value in $[a,b]$ across neighboring dataset pairs, the associated $\hat{p}$ ranges over $[0,1]$. Thus, an upper bound on $D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})||\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}'))$ that is valid over all neighboring datasets need to be obtained by maximizing $D_{\alpha}(p)$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_p} over $p\in [0,1]$.


Let $\Delta = b-a$ and define $A= e^{-\Delta}\in (0,1)$, $B= e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}>1$.  To maximize $D_\alpha(p)$ over $p\in[0,1]$,
take the first derivative on both sides with respect to $p$:
\begin{align}
    D'_{\alpha}(p) = \frac{dD_{\alpha}(p) }{dp} = \frac{A-1}{1-p+pA} + \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\cdot\frac{B-1}{1-p+pB}.
\end{align}
Setting $D'_{\alpha}(p)=0$, we have
\begin{align}
 p^*&=\arg\max_{p\in [0,1]} D_{\alpha}(p)\notag\\
&=\frac{(B\!-\!1)\!-\!(\alpha-1)(1-A)}{\alpha(1-A)(B-1)}\notag\\
&=\frac{ \big(e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}-1\big)\! -\! (\alpha\!-\!1)\big(1-e^{-\Delta}\big)}
{\alpha\big(1-e^{-\Delta}\big)\big(e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}-1\big)}.
\end{align}
Therefore,
\begin{align}
    D_{\alpha} &\le \log\left(1-p^*+p^*e^{-\Delta}\right)
    + \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(1-p^*+p^*e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}\right)\notag\\
    & =  \log\left(1-\frac{(B\!-\!1)\!-\!(\alpha-1)(1-A)}{\alpha(1-A)(B-1)}+\frac{(B\!-\!1)\!-\!(\alpha-1)(1-A)}{\alpha(1-A)(B-1)}A\right)\notag\\
    & \;\;\;\;+ \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(1-\frac{(B\!-\!1)\!-\!(\alpha-1)(1-A)}{\alpha(1-A)(B-1)}+\frac{(B\!-\!1)\!-\!(\alpha-1)(1-A)}{\alpha(1-A)(B-1)}B\right)\notag\\
    & =  \log\left(\frac{\alpha\cancel{(1-A)}(B-1)-\cancel{(1-A)}(B\!-\!1)\!+\!(\alpha-1)\cancel{(1-A)}\!-\!(\alpha-1)\cancel{(1-A)}A}{\alpha\cancel{(1-A)}(B-1)}\right)\notag\\
    & \;\;\;\;+ \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(\frac{\alpha(1-A)\cancel{(B-1)}-\cancel{(B-1)}+B\cancel{(B-1)}\!-\!(\alpha-1)(1-A)\cancel{(B-1)}}{\alpha(1-A)\cancel{(B-1)}}\right)\notag\\
    &= \log\left(\frac{(\alpha-1)(B-A)}{\alpha(B-1)}\right)
    +\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\!\left(\frac{B-A}{\alpha(1-A)}\right)\triangleq \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta).\label{eqn:eps_ub}
\end{align}
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
Let $X$ and $Y$ be two random variables with the same finite means. Then $X \le_{\mathrm{cx}} Y$ if and only if,
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}(\phi(X))\le \mathbb{E}(\phi(Y)) \qquad \text{for any convex function $\phi$}.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- claim-1: body_md -->
For $\forall \alpha > 1$, and for $\forall \Delta_2 \ge \Delta_1\ge0$, we have $0 \le \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta_2)- \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta_1)\le \Delta_2-\Delta_1$.
<!-- end claim-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Take the first derivative of Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_p} with respect to $\Delta$ 
\begin{align}
\partial_\Delta D_\alpha(p,\Delta)&=-\frac{p e^{-\Delta}}{1-p+pe^{-\Delta}}+\frac{p e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}}{1-p+pe^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}}= h\left(e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}\right) - h\left(e^{-\Delta}\right),
\end{align}
where $h(u)=\frac{pu}{1-p+pu}$ for $u>0$. Since
\begin{align}
    \frac{\partial h(u)}{\partial u} = \frac{p(1-p+pu)-p^2u}{(1-p+pu)^2} = \frac{p(1-p)}{(1-p+pu)^2}\ge 0,
\end{align}
$h(u)$ is non-decreasing in $u$. Per $\alpha > 1$ and $\Delta \ge 0$, $e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta} \ge e^{-\Delta}$, then $\partial_\Delta D_\alpha(p,\Delta) \ge 0$ and the equality holds only in the degenerate cases $p=0, p=1$, or $\Delta = 0$.

Since the pointwise maximum preserves order, for any $\Delta_2 \ge \Delta_1$ and $\forall p \in [0,1], \forall \alpha > 1$:
\begin{align}
    &0\le D_\alpha(p,\Delta_2)-D_\alpha(p,\Delta_1)\le \Delta_2-\Delta_1\notag\\
    &\text{Taking the maximum over $p$ on both sides}\notag\\
    \Rightarrow\; & 0 \le \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta_2)- \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta_1)\le \Delta_2-\Delta_1.
\end{align}
We can conclude that $ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta)$ is $1$-Lipschitz in $\Delta$. In other words, $ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta)$ grows  at most linearly with slope at most 1.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- claim-2: body_md -->
For fixed $\Delta$, as $\alpha \rightarrow \infty$, $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta)=\Delta-\alpha^{-1}\log\left(\alpha(1-e^{-\Delta})\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha^{-1}\right).$
<!-- end claim-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
$A=e^{-\Delta} \in (0,1)$. As $\alpha \rightarrow \infty, B = e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}\rightarrow \infty$. Thus
\begin{align}
 \frac{B-A}{B-1} = 1+\frac{1-A}{B-1} = 1+\mathcal{O}(B^{-1})=1+\mathcal{O}(e^{-(\alpha-1)\Delta})\label{eqn:1st_term_expansion}
\end{align}
Plug Eq.~\eqref{eqn:1st_term_expansion} into the first term in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_ub}
\begin{align}
\log\left(\frac{(\alpha-1)(B-A)}{\alpha(B-1)}\right) =& \log\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}\right) + \log\left(\frac{B-A}{B-1}\right)\notag\\
=& \log\left(1-\alpha^{-1}\right) + \log\left(1+\mathcal{O}(e^{-(\alpha-1)\Delta})\right)\notag\\
=& -\alpha^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{-2})+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-(\alpha-1)\Delta}\right)\label{eqn:2nd_term}\quad \text{as $\alpha\rightarrow\infty$}
\end{align}
Since $B-A = e^{(\alpha-1)\Delta}-e^{-\Delta} = e^{-\Delta}\big(e^{\alpha\Delta}-1\big)$, 
\begin{align}
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\!\left(\frac{B-A}{\alpha(1-A)}\right) =& \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\Big[\log\!\left(B-A\right)-\log(\alpha(1-A))\Big]\notag\\
=& \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\Big[-\Delta+\log\left(e^{\alpha\Delta}-1\right)-\log(\alpha(1-A))\Big]\notag\\
=& \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\Big[-\Delta+\alpha\Delta+\log\left(1-e^{-\alpha\Delta}\right)-\log(\alpha(1-A))\Big]\notag\\
=& \Delta - \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log(\alpha(1-A)) + \frac{\log\left(1-e^{-\alpha\Delta}\right)}{\alpha-1}\label{eqn:1st_term}
\end{align}
Plug Eqs.~\eqref{eqn:1st_term} and \eqref{eqn:2nd_term} in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_ub}, then 
    \begin{align}
        \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ub}}(\alpha, \Delta)  &= \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\!\left(\frac{B-A}{\alpha(1-A)}\right)+\log\left(\frac{(\alpha-1)(B-A)}{\alpha(B-1)}\right) \notag\\
        &= \Delta - \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log(\alpha(1-A)) + \frac{\log\left(1-e^{-\alpha\Delta}\right)}{\alpha-1}-\alpha^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{-2})+\underbrace{\text{ 
  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize

  \@setfontsize

  \@setfontsize 
  \@setfontsize\normalsize\@xpt\@xipt

\@xpt\@xipt

  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@

  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
 $\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-(\alpha-1)\Delta}\right)$}}_{\text{dominated by $\alpha^{-2}$}}\notag\\
        &\quad \text{since } \frac{1}{\alpha-1} = \frac{1}{\alpha} + \mathcal{O}(\alpha^{-2})\text{ and } \log\left(1-e^{-\alpha\Delta}\right) \approx e^{-\alpha\Delta}\quad\text{for sufficiently large } \alpha \notag\\
        &=\Delta - \left(\frac{1}{\alpha}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{-2})\right)\cdot\log(\alpha(1-A))+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{-\alpha\Delta}}{\alpha}\right)-\alpha^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{-2})\notag\\
        &\quad \text{since } \frac{e^{-\alpha\Delta}}{\alpha}\le\frac{1}{\alpha^2}
        \le\frac{\log\alpha}{\alpha^2}\le \frac{1}{\alpha}\le\frac{\log\alpha}{\alpha}
        \quad\text{for sufficiently large }\alpha\notag\\
        &=\Delta -\frac{1}{\alpha}\log(\alpha(1-e^{-\Delta}))+\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha^{-1}\right)
    \end{align}
Similar to Appendix~\ref{ape:uniform}, $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)\in[-b,-a]$ under $Q$, we also have $D_\alpha(Q\|P)\le b-a=\Delta$ for every $\alpha>1$. Hence $\max\{D_\infty(P\|Q),D_\infty(Q\|P)\}\le\Delta$, and the pure $\Delta$-DP conclusion of Corollary~5 is two-sided.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
\emph{Step 1: a fixed ordered pair.}
Fix an ordered neighboring pair $(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')$, and let $P=\pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})$, $Q=\pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}')$. Suppose $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)=\log\{\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y})/\ell(\boldsymbol\theta;\mathbf{Y}')\}$ is sub-Gaussian under $P$, i.e., there exists $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}\in(0,\infty)$ such that for all $t\in\mathbb R$,
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:subG_condition}
    \log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\!\left[e^{t(c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)-\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})}\right]\le \frac{t^2\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2},
    \qquad \mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}[c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)].
\end{equation}
Setting $t=\alpha-1$ and $t=-1$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:subG_condition}, respectively, bounds the two exponential moments in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_ape}:
\begin{align}
    \log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\big[e^{(\alpha-1)c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\big]&\le (\alpha-1)\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'} + \frac{(\alpha-1)^2\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2},\notag\\
    c_2=\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\big[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\big] &\le -\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}+\frac{\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2}.\notag
\end{align}
Plugging both into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape},
\begin{align}
    D_{\alpha}(P\|Q) &= c_2 +\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\big[e^{(\alpha-1)c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\big]\notag\\
    &\le -\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}+\frac{\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2}+\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'} + \frac{(\alpha-1)\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2}
    = \frac{\alpha}{2}\,\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}. \label{eqn:subG_pairwise}
\end{align}

\emph{Step 2: uniform, two-sided bound.}
Applying Step~1 to the reversed pair $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$ gives $D_\alpha(Q\|P)\le\frac{\alpha}{2}\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}$, where, by definition, $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}$ is the variance proxy of $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)=-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ under $Q$. Since $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$ is an ordered neighboring pair whenever $(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')$ is, both $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}$ and $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}$ are bounded by
\begin{equation}
\bar\sigma^2\triangleq\sup_{(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'):\,d(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')=1}\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'},
\end{equation}
where the supremum is over all ordered neighboring pairs in the admissible data domain. Hence, by Eq.~\eqref{eqn:subG_pairwise},
\begin{equation}
\sup_{(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'):\,d(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')=1}\max\{D_\alpha(P\|Q),D_\alpha(Q\|P)\}\le\frac{\alpha\bar\sigma^2}{2}\triangleq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{subGaus}}(\alpha,\bar\sigma^2),
\end{equation}
which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
If assume $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ is sub-Exponential under $\pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})$, i.e. there exist
$\nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2\in(0,\infty)$ and $b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}\in(0,\infty)$ such that for all $t\in\mathbb{R}$ satisfying
$|t|<1/b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}$, it holds that
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:subexp_con}
    \log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim P}\!\left(e^{t(c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)-\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})}\right)\le \frac{t^2\nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}|t|)};
\end{equation}
Then, by setting $t$ as $-1$ and $\alpha-1$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:subexp_con} respectively, the required exponential moments of $c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ under $\pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_ape} can be derived as:
\begin{align}
    &\text{when $t=\alpha-1$, we have: }\notag\\
    &\qquad \log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left(e^{(\alpha-1) \cdot c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right) \le (\alpha-1) \mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'} + \frac{(\alpha-1)^2\nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}(\alpha-1))}, \\
    &\text{when $t=-1$, we have: }\notag\\
    &\qquad c_2=\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot| \mathbf{Y})}[e^{-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}]\le -\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}+\frac{\nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2}{2(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})}.
\end{align}
Plugging the above two into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}, under $b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}<1$ and $b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}(\alpha-1)<1$,
\begin{align}
    D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y})\|\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')) 
    &= c_2 +\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)\cdot c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)}\right]\notag\\
    &\le -\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}+\frac{\nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2}{2(1\!-\!b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})}+\frac{1}{\alpha\!-\!1}\Big[(\alpha\!-\!1) \mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'} + \frac{(\alpha\!-\!1)^2\nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}(\alpha-1))}\Big]\notag\\
    &= \frac{\nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2}{2(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})}+ \frac{(\alpha-1)\nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{2(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}(\alpha-1))}\notag\\
    &= \frac{\nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2}{2}\cdot\frac{1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}(\alpha-1)+(\alpha-1)(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})}{(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}(\alpha-1))}\notag\\
    &= \frac{\nu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2}{2}
        \frac{\alpha(1-2b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})+2b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}}{(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})\bigl(1-b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}(\alpha-1)\bigr)} = \varepsilon_{\text{subExp}}(\alpha, b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}, \nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}). \label{eqn:subexp_pairwise}
\end{align}
Applying the above to the reversed pair $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$, i.e., to $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)=-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)$ under $\pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y}')$, gives $D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}')\|\pi(\boldsymbol\theta|\mathbf{Y}))\le\varepsilon_{\text{subExp}}(\alpha, b_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}, \nu^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}})$, provided $b_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}<\min\{(\alpha-1)^{-1},1\}$. Since $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$ is an ordered neighboring pair whenever $(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')$ is, if $b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}<\min\{(\alpha-1)^{-1},1\}$ for all ordered neighboring pairs, then
\begin{align}
\max\{D_\alpha(P\|Q),D_\alpha(Q\|P)\}\le\sup_{(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'):\,d(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}')=1}\varepsilon_{\text{subExp}}(\alpha, b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}, \nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'})
\end{align}
uniformly over all neighboring pairs, which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Let $y_i \in \{1,\ldots,K\} $ denote a categorical outcome with $K$ categories. Let $\boldsymbol\theta=(\theta_1,\dots,\theta_K)$, where $\sum_{j=1}^K \theta_j = 1$ and  $\theta_j=\Pr(y_i=j)$. Assume $y_i|\boldsymbol\theta\sim\text{Categorical}(\boldsymbol\theta)$ independently for $i=1,\ldots,n$, and the likelihood of the dataset $Y = \{y_1, \ldots, y_n\}$ is $\ell (\boldsymbol\theta; \mathbf{Y}) = \prod_{j=1}^K\theta_j^{s_j(Y)}$, where $s_j(Y) = \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{1}\{y_i=j\}$ is the category counts.

  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf\raggedright} 
{bounded neighboring}
WLOG, two neighboring datasets $Y$ and $Y'$ are both of size $n$ and differ in exactly one observation. Specifically, there exist distinct indices $j\neq k$ and $j, k \in \{1, \ldots, K\}$ such that
$s_j(Y)=s_j(Y')+1,
s_k(Y)=s_k(Y')-1$, and $s_\ell(Y)=s_\ell(Y')$ for all $\ell\notin\{j,k\}$.
\begin{align}
    &c_1(\boldsymbol\theta) = \log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};Y)}{\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};Y')} = \sum_{\ell=1}^K \big(s_\ell(Y)-s_\ell(Y')\big)\log\theta_\ell = \log\frac{\theta_j}{\theta_k}\\
    \Rightarrow\; &D^{(jk)}_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
    =\log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\frac{\theta_k}{\theta_j}\right]+
    \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\left(
    \log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(\frac{\theta_j}{\theta_k}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right]
    \right).\label{eqn:D_alpha_multinomial_bounded}
\end{align}
Finally, to obtain an RDP guarantee that holds for all neighboring datasets under bounded neighboring, one takes the maximum over all ordered pairs $(j,k)$ corresponding to a single-record change:
\begin{equation}
    D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot|Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big) = \max_{j,k\in \{1, \ldots, K\}, j\ne k}\Big\{D^{(jk)}_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}\big(\pi(\cdot|Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)\Big\},
\end{equation}
Since the maximum ranges over all ordered category pairs $(j,k)$ and all datasets $Y$ in the domain, the reverse ordering $(Y',Y)$, which is itself a bounded-neighboring pair with $j$ and $k$ exchanged and posterior $\pi(\cdot|Y')$, is already included.

\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@

  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Dirichlet prior.} Assume a Dirichlet prior:
$\pi(\boldsymbol\theta)\!=\!\mathrm{Dir}(\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_K).$ Then
$\boldsymbol\theta|Y \!\sim\! \mathrm{Dir}(\beta_1,\dots,\beta_K)$, where $\beta_j=\alpha_j+s_j(Y)$ for $j \in \{1, \ldots, K\}.$ We can calculate the moment below:
\begin{align}
    &\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\frac{\theta_k}{\theta_j}\right]
    =\frac{\beta_k}{\beta_j-1} = \frac{\alpha_k + s_k(Y)}{\alpha_j + s_j(Y)-1}, \text{ if $\beta_j > 1$}\label{eqn:dir_c2_bounded}\\
    &\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(\frac{\theta_j}{\theta_k}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right] =
    \frac{\Gamma(\beta_j+\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(\beta_j)}
    \cdot
    \frac{\Gamma(\beta_k-\alpha+1)}{\Gamma(\beta_k)}, \text{ if $\beta_k > \alpha-1$}
    \label{eqn:dir_c1_bounded}
\end{align}
Therefore, plug Eqns.~\eqref{eqn:dir_c1_bounded}\eqref{eqn:dir_c2_bounded}  into Eq~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_multinomial_bounded}, we can derive the closed-form expression for R\'enyi Divergence:
\begin{align}
D^{(jk)}_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot|Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
= \log\frac{\beta_k}{\beta_j-1}+
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\left[
\log\left(
\frac{\Gamma(\beta_j-1+\alpha)}{\Gamma(\beta_j)}
\cdot
\frac{\Gamma(\beta_k+1-\alpha)}{\Gamma(\beta_k)}
\right)
\right].\notag
\end{align}
Finally, with $\alpha < 1+\min_k \beta_k$,
\begin{equation}
    D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot|Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big) = \max_{j,k\in \{1, \ldots, K\}, j\ne k}\Big\{D^{(jk)}_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}\big(\pi(\cdot|Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)\Big\}.
\end{equation}

\bf} 
{Special case.} If $\exists \;\eta \in (0,1/2)$ such that $\forall j \in \{1, \ldots, K\}, \theta_j \in [\eta, 1-\eta]$, then$|c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)|\le \log(\frac{1-\eta}{\eta})$.

\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@

  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf\raggedright} 
{unbounded neighboring}
WLOG, let dataset $Y$ has size $n$ and dataset $Y'$ is of size $n-1$ by removing the $n$-th record $y_n\in \{1, \ldots, K\}$. Specifically, there exist one index $j\in \{1, \ldots, K\}$ such that
$s_j(Y)=s_j(Y')+1$, and $s_\ell(Y)=s_\ell(Y')$ for all $\ell\ne j$.
\begin{align}
    &c_1(\boldsymbol\theta) = \log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};Y)}{\ell(\boldsymbol{\theta};Y')} = \sum_{\ell=1}^K \big(s_\ell(Y)-s_\ell(Y')\big)\log\theta_\ell = \log\theta_j\\
    \Rightarrow\; &D^{(j)}_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
    =\log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\theta_j^{-1}\right]+
    \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\left(
    \log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\theta_j^{\alpha-1}\right]
    \right).\label{eqn:D_alpha_multinomial}
\end{align}
For the reverse direction, $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)=-c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)=-\log\theta_j$, and by Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_reverse},
\begin{align}
    D^{(j)}_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot| Y')\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y)\big)
    =\log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y')}\!\left[\theta_j\right]+
    \frac{1}{\alpha-1}
    \log \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y')}\!\left[\theta_j^{-(\alpha-1)}\right].\label{eqn:D_alpha_multinomial_rev}
\end{align}


Finally, to obtain an RDP guarantee that holds for all neighboring datasets under unbounded neighboring, one takes the maximum over all $j$ :
\begin{align*}
    D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot|Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big) = \max_{j\in \{1, \ldots, K\}}\max\Big\{&D^{(j)}_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot|Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big),\\
    &D^{(j)}_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot|Y')\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y)\big)\Big\},
\end{align*}

  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@

  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Dirichlet prior.} Assume a Dirichlet prior:
$\pi(\boldsymbol\theta)=\mathrm{Dir}(\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_K).$ Then
$\boldsymbol\theta|Y \sim \mathrm{Dir}(\beta_1,\dots,\beta_K)$, where $\beta_j=\alpha_j+s_j(Y)$ for $j \in \{1, \ldots, K\}.$ And the marginal $\theta_j|Y\sim\text{Beta}(\beta_j, \sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell-\beta_j)$, based on which, we can calculate the moments below:
\begin{align}
    &\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\left[\theta_j^{-1}\right]
    =\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell-1}{\beta_j-1} \text{ if $\beta_j > 1$}\label{eqn:dir_c2}\\
    &\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\theta_j^{\alpha-1}\right] =
    \frac{\Gamma(\beta_j+\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(\beta_j)}
    \cdot
    \frac{\Gamma(\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell)}{\Gamma(\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell+\alpha-1)}.
    \label{eqn:dir_c1}
\end{align}
Therefore, plug Eqns.~\eqref{eqn:dir_c1}\eqref{eqn:dir_c2}  into Eq~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_multinomial}, we can derive the closed-form expression for R\'enyi Divergence:

\begin{align}
D^{(j)}_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot|Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
= \log \frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell-1}{\beta_j-1}+
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left[\frac{\Gamma(\beta_j+\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(\beta_j)}
    \cdot
    \frac{\Gamma(\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell)}{\Gamma(\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell+\alpha-1)}
\right].
\end{align}}
Since $s_j(Y)\ge1$, $\beta_j\ge\alpha_j+1>1$, so this direction is finite for every $\alpha>1$.
For the reverse direction, $\theta_j|Y'\sim\text{Beta}(\beta_j-1, \sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell-\beta_j)$, when $1<\alpha<\beta_j$

\begin{align}
D^{(j)}_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}\!\big(\pi(\cdot|Y')\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y)\big)
= \log\frac{\beta_j-1}{\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell-1}+
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left[\frac{\Gamma(\beta_j-\alpha)}{\Gamma(\beta_j-1)}
    \cdot
    \frac{\Gamma(\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell-1)}{\Gamma(\sum_{\ell=1}^K\beta_\ell-\alpha)}\right],
\end{align}}
but $=\infty$ for $\alpha\ge\beta_j$. Since $\beta_j\ge\alpha_j+1$ with equality when $s_j(Y)=1$, the two-sided divergence is finite uniformly over all unbounded neighboring pairs if and only if $\alpha<1+\min_j\alpha_j$; hence OPS with a Dirichlet prior does not satisfy pure DP under unbounded neighboring.

\bf} 
{Special Case.} If $\exists \;\eta \in (0,1/2)$ such that $\forall j \in \{1, \ldots, K\}, \theta_j \in [\eta, 1-\eta]$, then $|c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)|\le \log(\frac{1}{\eta})$. In the reverse direction, $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\theta)=-\log\theta_j$ has a range of the same width, so Corollary~\ref{coro:uniform} applies in both directions.

Assume observed dataset $Y = \{y_i\}_{i=1}^n$, where $y_i|\theta \overset{\text{i.i.d}}{\sim}\text{Bernoulli}(\theta)$ with $\theta=\Pr(y_i=1)$ and $1-\theta=\Pr(y_i=0)$.

  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@

  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf\raggedright} 
{bounded neighboring}
WLOG, suppose neighboring datasets $Y,Y'$, both of size $n$, differ in their $n$-th record $y_n$ and $y'_n$. Then the log-likelihood ratio can be derived as
\begin{align}
    &\ell(\theta;Y)=\theta^{y_n+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} y_i}(1-\theta)^{n-y_n-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} y_i}\notag\\
    &\ell(\theta;Y')=\theta^{y'_n+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} y_i}(1-\theta)^{n-y'_n-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} y_i}\notag\\
    \Rightarrow \;&c_1(\theta)=\log\frac{\ell(\theta;Y)}{\ell(\theta;Y')}
    =(y_n-y'_n)\log\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right) = \begin{cases}
        \log\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right) & \text{if $y_n -y'_n = 1$}\\
        -\log\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right) & \text{if $y_n -y'_n = -1$}
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
If $y_n - y'_n = 1$ with $\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}\!\left[\frac{1-\theta}{\theta}\right]< \infty$ and $\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right]<\infty$:
\begin{align}
D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}^{(+)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
= \log\mathbb{E}_{\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|Y)}\!\left[\frac{1\!-\!\theta}{\theta}\right] + 
\frac{1}{\alpha\!-\!1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(\frac{\theta}{1\!-\!\theta}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right].\notag
\end{align}
If $y_n - y'_n = -1$ with $\mathbb{E}_{\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|Y)}\!\left[\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right]< \infty$ and $\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(\frac{1-\theta}{\theta}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right]<\infty$:
\begin{align}
D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}^{(-)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
= \log\mathbb{E}_{\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|Y)}\left[\frac{\theta}{1\!-\!\theta}\right] + 
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(\frac{1\!-\!\theta}{\theta}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right].\notag
\end{align}
Without additional structure on the posterior, the worst-case R\'enyi divergence over bounded neighboring is given by
\begin{equation}
    D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)\le\max\{D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}^{(+)},\,D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}^{(-)}\}\notag
\end{equation}
The reverse ordering $(Y',Y)$ is itself a bounded-neighboring pair with $y'_n-y_n=\mp1$ and posterior $\pi(\cdot|Y')$, i.e., it is of the form $D^{(\mp)}_{\alpha,\text{bounded}}$ evaluated at the dataset $Y'$. Hence taking the maximum over $D^{(+)}$ and $D^{(-)}$ and over all datasets in the domain already covers the reverse direction.

\bf} 
{Special case.} If $\theta\in [\eta, 1-\eta]$ for some constant $\eta \in (0,1/2)$. Since $\log(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta})$ is monotonically increasing, $|c_1(\theta)|\le \log(\frac{1-\eta}{\eta})$ uniformly. 

 
  \@startsection{paragraph}{4}{\z@}

\@xpt\@xipt

  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@

  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Beta prior.} If $\pi(\theta) = \text{Beta}(a, b)$, and define $s = \sum_{i=1}^n y_i$,  then $\pi(\theta|Y) = \text{Beta}(A, B)$, where $A = a+s$, $B = b+n-s$. \\
If $y_n - y'_n = 1$ and  $A > 1, B > \alpha-1$
\begin{align}
&\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\frac{1-\theta}{\theta}\right]
=\frac{B}{A-1}\notag\\
&\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right]
=
\frac{\text{Beta}(A-1+\alpha,B+1-\alpha)}{\text{Beta}(A,B)} = \frac{\Gamma(A+\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(A)}\cdot
\frac{\Gamma(B-(\alpha-1))}{\Gamma(B)}\notag\\
                                                              
\Rightarrow \; &D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}^{(+)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
= \log\left[\frac{B}{A-1}\right] + \frac{1}{\alpha-1}
\log \left[\frac{\Gamma(A+\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(A)}\cdot
\frac{\Gamma(B-(\alpha-1))}{\Gamma(B)}\right].
\end{align}
If $y_n - y'_n = -1$, and $B>1, A>\alpha-1$
\begin{align}
    &\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right]
    =\frac{A}{B-1}\notag\\
    &\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(\frac{1-\theta}{\theta}\right)^{\alpha-1}\right]=
    \frac{\text{Beta}(A+1-\alpha,B-1+\alpha)}{\text{Beta}(A,B)}= \frac{\Gamma(A-(\alpha-1))}{\Gamma(A)}\cdot
    \frac{\Gamma(B+(\alpha-1))}{\Gamma(B)}\notag\\
                                                                  
    \Rightarrow \;& D_{\alpha, \text{bounded}}^{(-)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
    = \log\left[\frac{A}{B-1}\right] + \frac{1}{\alpha-1}
    \log \left[\frac{\Gamma(A-(\alpha-1))}{\Gamma(A)}\cdot
    \frac{\Gamma(B+(\alpha-1))}{\Gamma(B)}\right] \notag\\
\end{align}

\begin{remark}
    To ensure finite Beta-moment, $\alpha < \alpha_{\max} = 1 + \min\{a+s,\; b+n-s\}$.
\end{remark}

\bf\raggedright} 
{unbounded neighboring}
WLOG, suppose neighboring datasets $Y,Y'$, where $Y$ is of size $n$ and $Y'$ is of size $n-1$ by removing the $n$-th record $y_n$. Then similarly, the log-likelihood ratio can be derived as
\begin{align}
    &\ell(\theta;Y)=\theta^{y_n+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} y_i}(1-\theta)^{n-y_n-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} y_i}\notag\\
    &\ell(\theta;Y')=\theta^{\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} y_i}(1-\theta)^{n-1-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1} y_i}\notag\\
    \Rightarrow \;&c_1(\theta)=\log\frac{\ell(\theta;Y)}{\ell(\theta;Y')}
    =y_n\log\theta+(1-y_n)\log(1-\theta) = \begin{cases}
        \log\theta & \text{if $y_n= 1$}\\
        \log\left(1-\theta\right) & \text{if $y_n = 0$}
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
If $y_n =1$,
\begin{align}
D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(1)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\|\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
= \log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|Y)}\left[\theta^{-1}\right]+ 
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\theta^{\alpha-1}\right].\notag
\end{align}
If $y_n = 0$,
\begin{align}
D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(0)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\,\|\,\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
= \log\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|Y)}\left[(1-\theta)^{-1}\right]+ 
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(1-\theta\right)^{\alpha-1}\right].\notag
\end{align}
For the reverse direction, $\tilde c_1(\theta)=-c_1(\theta)$ and the expectations are taken under $\pi(\cdot|Y')$:
\begin{align}
D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(1)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y')\|\pi(\cdot| Y)\big)
&= \log\mathbb{E}_{\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|Y')}\left[\theta\right]+ 
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y')}\!\left[\theta^{-(\alpha-1)}\right],\notag\\
D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(0)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y')\|\pi(\cdot| Y)\big)
&= \log\mathbb{E}_{\theta \sim \pi(\cdot|Y')}\left[1-\theta\right]+ 
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y')}\!\left[(1-\theta)^{-(\alpha-1)}\right].\notag
\end{align}
Without additional structure on the posterior, the worst-case R\'enyi divergence over unbounded neighboring is given by
\begin{equation}
    D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}\le\max_{k\in\{0,1\}}\max\Big\{D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(k)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\|\pi(\cdot| Y')\big),\,D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(k)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y')\|\pi(\cdot| Y)\big)\Big\}\notag
\end{equation}
\textbf{Special case:} If $\theta\in [\eta, 1-\eta]$ for some $\eta \in (0,1/2)$. $|c_1(\theta)|\le \max\{|\log\eta|, |\log(1-\eta)|\} = -\log\eta$ uniformly. In the reverse direction, $\tilde c_1(\theta)=-c_1(\theta)$ satisfies the same bound, so Corollary~\ref{coro:uniform} applies in both directions.

\bf} 
{Beta prior.} If $\pi(\theta) = \text{Beta}(a, b)$, and define $s = \sum_{i=1}^n y_i$,  then $\pi(\theta|Y) = \text{Beta}(A, B)$, where $A = a+s$, $B = b+n-s$. Then \\
If $y_n =1$ and  $A > 1$,
\begin{align}
    &\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\theta^{-1}\right]
    =\frac{A+B-1}{A-1}; \\
    &\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\theta^{\alpha-1}\right]
    =
    \frac{\text{Beta}(A-1+\alpha,B)}{\text{Beta}(A,B)}= \frac{\Gamma(A+\alpha-1)\Gamma(A+B)}{\Gamma(A)\,\Gamma(A+B+\alpha-1)}\\
    \Rightarrow \; &D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(1)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\|\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
= \log\left(\frac{A+B-1}{A-1}\right)+ 
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \left(\frac{\Gamma(A+\alpha-1)\Gamma(A+B)}{\Gamma(A)\,\Gamma(A+B+\alpha-1)}\right).
\end{align}
If $y_n=0$, and $B>1$,
\begin{align}
    &\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[(1-\theta)^{-1}\right]
    =\frac{A+B-1}{B-1};\\
    &\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi(\cdot| Y)}\!\left[\left(1-\theta\right)^{\alpha-1}\right]
    =
    \frac{\text{Beta}(A,B-1+\alpha)}{\text{Beta}(A,B)}=\frac{\Gamma(A+B)\Gamma(B+\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(A+B+\alpha-1)\Gamma(B)}\\
    \Rightarrow \; &D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(0)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y)\|\pi(\cdot| Y')\big)
    = \log\left(\frac{A+B-1}{B-1}\right)+ 
    \frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \left(\frac{\Gamma(B+\alpha-1)\Gamma(A+B)}{\Gamma(B)\,\Gamma(A+B+\alpha-1)}\right).
\end{align}
For the reverse direction, if $y_n=1$, then $\pi(\theta|Y')=\text{Beta}(A-1,B)$ and if $1<\alpha<A$,
\begin{align}
D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(1)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y')\|\pi(\cdot| Y)\big)
= \log\left(\frac{A-1}{A+B-1}\right)+ 
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \left(\frac{\Gamma(A-\alpha)\Gamma(A+B-1)}{\Gamma(A-1)\,\Gamma(A+B-\alpha)}\right);
\end{align}
if $y_n=0$, then $\pi(\theta|Y')=\text{Beta}(A,B-1)$ and if $1<\alpha<B$.
\begin{align}
D_{\alpha, \text{unbounded}}^{(0)}\big(\pi(\cdot| Y')\|\pi(\cdot| Y)\big)
= \log\left(\frac{B-1}{A+B-1}\right)+ 
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \left(\frac{\Gamma(B-\alpha)\Gamma(A+B-1)}{\Gamma(B-1)\,\Gamma(A+B-\alpha)}\right).
\end{align}
Both equal $\infty$ when $\alpha$ exceeds the stated range.
\begin{remark}
    The forward direction under unbounded neighboring imposes no upper bound on the R\'enyi order $\alpha$, but the reverse direction is finite only for $\alpha<A$ (if $y_n=1$) or $\alpha<B$ (if $y_n=0$). Since $A=a+s\ge a+1$ when $y_n=1$ and $B=b+n-s\ge b+1$ when $y_n=0$, with equality attainable, the two-sided R\'enyi divergence is finite uniformly over all unbounded neighboring pairs if and only if $\alpha<1+\min\{a,b\}$. In particular, OPS with a Beta prior does not satisfy pure DP under unbounded neighboring.
\end{remark}
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
To ensure finite Beta-moment, $\alpha < \alpha_{\max} = 1 + \min\{a+s,\; b+n-s\}$.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
The forward direction under unbounded neighboring imposes no upper bound on the R\'enyi order $\alpha$, but the reverse direction is finite only for $\alpha<A$ (if $y_n=1$) or $\alpha<B$ (if $y_n=0$). Since $A=a+s\ge a+1$ when $y_n=1$ and $B=b+n-s\ge b+1$ when $y_n=0$, with equality attainable, the two-sided R\'enyi divergence is finite uniformly over all unbounded neighboring pairs if and only if $\alpha<1+\min\{a,b\}$. In particular, OPS with a Beta prior does not satisfy pure DP under unbounded neighboring.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We consider generalized linear models (GLMs) with canonical link. Each record is $(\mathbf{x}_i,y_i)$, where $\mathbf{x}_i\in\mathbb R^d$ and
$y_i\in\mathcal Y\subseteq\mathbb R$.
The dataset is $\mathbf{Y}=\{(\mathbf{x}_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$, and the model parameter is
$\boldsymbol\beta\in\mathbb R^d$. Conditioned on $\mathbf{x}_i$ and $\boldsymbol\beta$, the response $y_i$ follows an exponential family distribution as below:
\begin{equation}
p(y_i|\mathbf{x}_i,\boldsymbol\beta)=
\exp\!\left(y_i\,\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta-\psi(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)+
h(y_i)
\right),
\end{equation}
where $\psi(\cdot)$ is the log-partition function and $h(\cdot)$ does not depend on $\boldsymbol\beta$. Then the log-likelihood is
\begin{align}
    &\log p(y_i|\mathbf{x}_i,\boldsymbol\beta)=y_i\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta-\psi(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)+h(y_i)\\
    \Rightarrow\; & \ell(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})=\sum_{i=1}^n \log p(y_i|\mathbf{x}_i,\boldsymbol\beta)=\sum_{i=1}^n\left(y_i\,\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta-\psi(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\right)+\sum_{i=1}^n h(y_i). 
\end{align}
Suppose there exists a finite constant $L>0$ such that for every \((\mathbf{x}_i,y_i)\) and every \(\boldsymbol\beta\), $\left\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta}\log p(y_i| \mathbf{x}_i,\boldsymbol\beta)\right\|\le L$. If imposing a Gaussian prior on $\boldsymbol\beta$, i.e. $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta)= \mathcal N(0,\tau^2 \mathbf{I}_d)$, then the posterior distribution can be derived as
\begin{equation}
\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})\propto\exp\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^n\bigl(
y_i\,\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta-\psi(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\bigr)-
\frac{\|\boldsymbol\beta\|_2^2}{2\tau^2}\right).
\end{equation}

  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf\raggedright} 
{bounded neighboring}
Let $\mathbf{Y}$ and $\mathbf{Y}'$ differ in exactly one record, i.e. $\mathbf{z}_n=(\mathbf{x}_n,y_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}$ is replaced by $\mathbf{z}'_n=(\mathbf{x}'_n,y'_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}'$, then
\begin{align}
    c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=\log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y})}{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y}')}
    &=\log p(y_n|\mathbf{x}_n,\boldsymbol\beta) - \log p(y'_n|\mathbf{x}'_n,\boldsymbol\beta)\notag\\
    &= y_n\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta-\psi(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)+h(y_n) - \left(y'_n\mathbf{x}_n'^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta-\psi(\mathbf{x}_n'^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta)+h(y'_n)\right)\notag\\
    &= y_n\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta-y'_n\mathbf{x}_n'^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta-\left(\psi(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)-\psi(\mathbf{x}_n'^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta)\right)+h(y_n) - h(y'_n)\label{eqn:c1_GLM_bounded}
\end{align}
First, we prove $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Lipschitz.
\begin{align}
    \nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta} c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)
    &= y_n \mathbf{x}_n - y_n' \mathbf{x}_n' - \left(\psi'(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n - \psi'(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}'_n\right)\\
    \Rightarrow \|\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta} c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)\|
    &= \Big\|y_n \mathbf{x}_n - y_n' \mathbf{x}_n' - \left(\psi'(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n - \psi'(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}'_n\right)\Big\|\notag\\
    &= \Big\|\left(y_n-\psi'(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\right) \mathbf{x}_n - \left( y_n' - \psi'(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\right) \mathbf{x}_n' \Big\|\notag\\
    & \le |y_n-\psi'(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)|\|\mathbf{x}_n\| + |y_n' - \psi'(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)| \|\mathbf{x}_n'\|\le 2L
\end{align}
Hence, $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Lipschitz continuous with constant $2L$. Then, we continue to prove the log-concavity of the posterior $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$.
\begin{align}
    \log \pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}) &= \sum_{i=1}^n\bigl(
    y_i\,\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta-\psi(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\bigr)-
    \frac{\|\boldsymbol\beta\|_2^2}{2\tau^2} + \text{constant}\\
    \Rightarrow -\nabla^2_{\boldsymbol\beta} \log \pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}) &=\sum_{i=1}^n \psi''(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_i \mathbf{x}_i^\top
    +\frac{1}{\tau^2}\mathbf{I}_d \succeq \frac{1}{\tau^2}\mathbf{I}_d \quad \text{per convexity of $\psi(\cdot)$}.
\end{align}
So $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$ is $m$-strongly log-concave with $m=\tau^{-2}$.

\begin{lemma}[Log-Sobolev Inequality \citep{ledoux2001concentration}]
Let $\pi$ be a probability measure on $\mathbb R^d$ with density
$\pi(x)\propto e^{-U(x)}$, where $U$ is twice continuously differentiable and
$m$-strongly convex, i.e.,
$\nabla^2 U(x)\succeq m I_d$ for all $x$.
Then $\pi$ satisfies a logarithmic Sobolev inequality (LSI) with constant $1/m$:
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\pi(g^2)\le\frac{2}{m}\,\mathbb E_\pi\!\left[\|\nabla g\|^2\right],
\quad
\text{for all smooth } g.
\]
\end{lemma}


\begin{lemma}[Herbst's Theorem \citep{ledoux2001concentration}]
Let $\pi$ be a probability measure satisfying a LSI
with constant $C_{\mathrm{LSI}}$.
Then for any $L$-Lipschitz function $f$,
\[
\log \mathbb E_\pi\!\left[
\exp\!\big(t(f-\mathbb E_\pi f)\big)
\right]\le
\frac{C_{\mathrm{LSI}}\,L^2\,t^2}{2},
\quad
\forall t\in\mathbb R.
\]
In particular, $f$ is sub-Gaussian under $\pi$ with variance proxy
$C_{\mathrm{LSI}}L^2$.
\end{lemma}
Per above lemmas, $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is sub-Gaussian under $ \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})$ with
\begin{align}
\sigma_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2\le \frac{(2L)^2}{m} = 4L^2\tau^2.
\end{align}
The same argument applies to the reversed pair $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$: $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=-c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ has the same Lipschitz constant $2L$, and $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')$ is also $\tau^{-2}$-strongly log-concave since $\mathbf{Y}'$ also has $n$ records. Hence $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}\le4L^2\tau^2$, and $\bar\sigma^2\le4L^2\tau^2$.
Plug into Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian}
\begin{align}
\max\{D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')),\,D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}))\} \le \frac{\alpha\bar{\sigma}^2}{2}\le 2\alpha L^2\tau^2
\end{align}
