\bf\raggedright} 
{unbounded neighboring}
Let $\mathbf{Y}$ has size $n$ and $\mathbf{Y}'$ is of size $n-1$ by removing the $n$-th record $\mathbf{z}_n=(\mathbf{x}_n,y_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}$. Then
\begin{align}
    c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=\log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y})}{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y}')}
    &=\log p(y_n|\mathbf{x}_n,\boldsymbol\beta)= y_n\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta-\psi(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)+h(y_n).\label{eqn:c1_GLM_unbounded}
\end{align}
Similarly, we prove $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Lipschitz.
\begin{align}
    \nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta} c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)
    &= y_n \mathbf{x}_n - \psi'(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n\notag\\
    \Rightarrow \|\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta} c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)\|
    &= \Big\|\left(y_n - \psi'(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\right)\mathbf{x}_n\Big\|\le L.
\end{align}
Combined with log-concavity, $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is sub-Gaussian under $ \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})$ with
\begin{align}
    \sigma_{\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'}^2\le \frac{L^2}{m}
    \le L^2\tau^2.
                   
                                           
                                                     
                 
\end{align}
For the reversed pair $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$, $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=-\log p(y_n|\mathbf{x}_n,\boldsymbol\beta)$ has the same gradient norm bound $L$, and
\begin{align}
-\nabla^2_{\boldsymbol\beta}\log\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\psi''(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_i\mathbf{x}_i^\top+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\mathbf I_d\succeq\frac{1}{\tau^2}\mathbf I_d,
\end{align}
where the empty sum equals $0$ when $n=1$. Hence $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')$ is also $\tau^{-2}$-strongly log-concave, $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}\le L^2\tau^2$, and $\bar\sigma^2\le L^2\tau^2$.
Plug into Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian}
\begin{align}
    \max\{D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')),\,D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}))\} &\le \frac{\alpha\bar{\sigma}^2}{2}\le \frac{\alpha L^2\tau^2}{2}.
\end{align}
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
Let $\pi$ be a probability measure on $\mathbb R^d$ with density
$\pi(x)\propto e^{-U(x)}$, where $U$ is twice continuously differentiable and
$m$-strongly convex, i.e.,
$\nabla^2 U(x)\succeq m I_d$ for all $x$.
Then $\pi$ satisfies a logarithmic Sobolev inequality (LSI) with constant $1/m$:
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\pi(g^2)\le\frac{2}{m}\,\mathbb E_\pi\!\left[\|\nabla g\|^2\right],
\quad
\text{for all smooth } g.
\]
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
Let $\pi$ be a probability measure satisfying a LSI
with constant $C_{\mathrm{LSI}}$.
Then for any $L$-Lipschitz function $f$,
\[
\log \mathbb E_\pi\!\left[
\exp\!\big(t(f-\mathbb E_\pi f)\big)
\right]\le
\frac{C_{\mathrm{LSI}}\,L^2\,t^2}{2},
\quad
\forall t\in\mathbb R.
\]
In particular, $f$ is sub-Gaussian under $\pi$ with variance proxy
$C_{\mathrm{LSI}}L^2$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
For logistic regression we have $\psi(t) = \log(1+e^t)$, and $h(\cdot)=0$, plug into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:c1_GLM_bounded}:
\begin{align}
    c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)&= y_n\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta-y'_n\mathbf{x}_n'^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta-\left(\log(1+e^{\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta})-\log(1+e^{\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta})\right)
\end{align}
First, we prove $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Lipschitz. Recall $\|\mathbf{x}\|\le R$ and let $\sigma(t) = \frac{1}{1+e^{-t}}$, given $|\sigma(\cdot)| < 1$ and $\frac{d \log(1+e^t)}{dt} = \sigma(t)$, we can have
\begin{align}
    \nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta} c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)
    &= y_n \mathbf{x}_n - y_n' \mathbf{x}_n'
    -\Big(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n - \sigma(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n'\Big)\notag\\
    & = \begin{cases}
    (y_n-y'_n)\mathbf{x}_n&\text{ if }\mathbf{x}_n = \mathbf{x}'_n \text{ and } y_n \ne y'_n\\
    y_n \mathbf{x}_n - y_n \mathbf{x}_n'
    -\Big(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n - \sigma(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n'\Big) & \text{ if }\mathbf{x}_n \ne \mathbf{x}'_n \text{ and } y_n = y'_n\\
    y_n \mathbf{x}_n - y_n' \mathbf{x}_n'
    -\Big(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n - \sigma(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n'\Big) & \text{ if }\mathbf{x}_n \ne \mathbf{x}'_n \text{ and } y_n \ne y'_n\\
    0  & \text{ if }\mathbf{x}_n = \mathbf{x}'_n \text{ and } y_n = y'_n\\
    \end{cases}.\notag
\end{align}
Therefore, when $\mathbf{x}_n = \mathbf{x}'_n$, the worst case is indeed flipping the label, then $\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta}  c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)\| = \|(y_n-y'_n)\mathbf{x}_n\|\le \|\mathbf{x}_n\|\le R$. Otherwise,
\begin{align}
    \|\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta}  c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)\|
    &\le \|y_n \mathbf{x}_n - y_n' \mathbf{x}_n'
    -\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n + \sigma(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n'\|\notag\\
    &\le \|(y_n-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)) \mathbf{x}_n - (y_n'-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)) \mathbf{x}_n'\|\notag\\
    &\le \|(y_n-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)) \mathbf{x}_n\| +\| (y_n'-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)) \mathbf{x}_n'\|\notag\\
    &\le\underbrace{|y_n-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)|}_{\le 1}\cdot\| \mathbf{x}_n\| +\underbrace{| y_n'-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)|}_{\le 1}\cdot\| \mathbf{x}_n'\|\le 2R.
\end{align}
Now we have proved that $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Lipschitz continuous with constant $L=R$ or $L=2R$. Then, we similarly prove the log-concavity of the posterior $\pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})$.
\begin{align}
    \log \pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}) &= \sum_{i=1}^n\bigl(
    y_i\,\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta-\log(1+e^{\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta})\bigr)-
    \frac{\|\boldsymbol\beta\|_2^2}{2\tau^2} + \text{constant}\notag\\
    \Rightarrow -\nabla^2_{\boldsymbol\beta} \log \pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}) &=\sum_{i=1}^n \sigma(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta)(1-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta))\mathbf{x}_i \mathbf{x}_i^\top
    +\frac{1}{\tau^2}\mathbf{I}_d \succeq \frac{1}{\tau^2}\mathbf{I}_d \text{ for $\forall \boldsymbol\beta$ }.
\end{align}
So $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$ is $m$-strongly log-concave with $m=\tau^{-2}$. Therefore,
\begin{align}
    \sigma_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}^2\le \frac{L^2}{m}\le
    \begin{cases}
        R^2\tau^2&\text{ if }\mathbf{x}_n=\mathbf{x}'_n\\
        (2R)^2\tau^2=4R^2\tau^2 &\text{ if } \mathbf{x}_n \ne \mathbf{x}'_n
    \end{cases}.
\end{align}
For the reversed pair $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$, the Lipschitz constants $R$ (if $\mathbf{x}_n=\mathbf{x}'_n$) and $2R$ (if $\mathbf{x}_n\ne\mathbf{x}'_n$) are symmetric in the two records, and $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')$ is also $\tau^{-2}$-strongly log-concave, so the same bounds hold for $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}$.
Plug into Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian}
\begin{align}
    \max\{D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')),\,D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}))\}&\le \frac{\alpha\bar{\sigma}^2}{2}\le   \begin{cases}
        \frac{1}{2}\alpha R^2\tau^2&\text{ if }\mathbf{x}_n=\mathbf{x}'_n\\
        2\alpha R^2\tau^2 &\text{ if } \mathbf{x}_n \ne \mathbf{x}'_n
    \end{cases}.
\end{align}
where $\bar\sigma^2$ is taken over the corresponding class of neighboring pairs.

For comparison, Theorem 16 in \citep{geumlek2017renyi} gives $(\alpha,2\alpha R^2\tau^2)$-RDP for the replacement case with $\mathbf{x}_n=\mathbf{x}'_n$; our bound for this case is $\alpha R^2\tau^2/2$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Now we have $\psi(t) = \log(1+e^t)$, and $h(\cdot)=0$.
Similarly, we prove $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Lipschitz first as what we did in bounded neighboring. Recall $\|\mathbf{x}\|\le R$ and let $\sigma(t) = \frac{1}{1+e^{-t}}$, given $y\in\{0,1\}, |\sigma(t)| < 1$ for $\forall t \in \mathbb R$ and $\frac{d \log(1+e^t)}{dt} = \sigma(t)$, we have
\begin{align}
    &\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta} c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)
    = y_n \mathbf{x}_n 
    -\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)\mathbf{x}_n\quad \text{ per Eq.~\eqref{eqn:c1_GLM_unbounded}}\notag\\
    \Rightarrow\;& \|\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta}  c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)\|= \|(y_n-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta))\mathbf{x}_n\| \le \underbrace{|y_n-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)|}_{\le 1}\|\mathbf{x}_n\|\le R
\end{align}
Now we have proved that $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Lipschitz continuous with constant $L=R$. Given $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$ is $m$-strongly log-concave with $m=\tau^{-2}$ and the Lemmas, $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is sub-Gaussian under $ \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})$ with
\begin{align}
    \sigma_{\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'}^2\le \frac{L^2}{m}\le R^2\tau^2
\end{align}
For the reversed pair $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$, $\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol\beta}\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)\|=|y_n-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)|\,\|\mathbf{x}_n\|\le R$, and $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')$ is $\tau^{-2}$-strongly log-concave by the argument for general GLMs above. Hence $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}\le R^2\tau^2$ and $\bar\sigma^2\le R^2\tau^2$.
Plug into Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian}
\begin{align}
\max\{D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')),\,D_{\alpha}(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}))\}&\le \frac{\alpha\bar{\sigma}^2}{2}\le \frac{\alpha R^2\tau^2}{2}
\end{align}
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Assume observed dataset $Y = \{y_i\}_{i=1}^n$, where $y_i|\theta \overset{\text{i.i.d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\theta, \sigma^2)$ and $\sigma^2$ is known. If imposing a Gaussian prior on $\theta$, i.e. $\pi(\theta)= \mathcal N(\mu_0,\tau^2)$, then per conjugacy, the posterior $\theta|Y \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_Y, v_Y^2)$, where 
\begin{align}\label{eqn:GG_post_para}
v^2_Y=\left(\frac{n}{\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\right)^{-1},
\qquad
\mu_Y=v^2_Y\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n y_i+\frac{\mu_0}{\tau^2}\right).
\end{align}

\bf\raggedright} 
{bounded neighboring}
WLOG, suppose neighboring datasets $Y,Y'$, both of size $n$, differ in their $n$-th record $y_n$ and $y'_n$. Then the log-likelihood ratio $c_1(\theta)$ can be derived as
\begin{align}
    \ell(\theta;Y)&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left({-\frac{(y_n-\theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{(y_i-\theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}}\right);\\
    \ell(\theta;Y')&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2}\exp\left({-\frac{(y'_n-\theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{(y_i-\theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}}\right)\\
    \Rightarrow \;c_1(\theta)&=\log\frac{\ell(\theta;Y)}{\ell(\theta;Y')}
    =\frac{(y'_n-\theta)^2-(y_n-\theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}\notag\\
    &= \frac{(y_n'^2-y_n^2)-2\theta(y_n'-y_n)}{2\sigma^2} \triangleq K+\frac{\Delta y}{\sigma^2}\theta,
\end{align}
where $K = \frac{(y_n'^2-y_n^2)}{2\sigma^2}$ and $\Delta y=y_n-y'_n$. Per the affine invariance of Gaussian distributions
\begin{align}
    c_1(\theta)\sim \mathcal{N}\left(K + \frac{\Delta y}{\sigma^2}\mu_Y, \left(\frac{\Delta y}{\sigma^2}\right)^2 v^2_Y\right).
\end{align}
Since the moment generating function (MGF) for $\mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma^2)$ is $\exp({t\mu +\sigma ^{2}t^{2}/2})$,
\begin{align}
    &\log\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim \pi(\theta|Y)}\left(e^{t(c_1(\theta)-\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim \pi(\theta|Y)}\left[c_1(\theta)\right])}\right)\notag\\
    =\;&
    \frac{t^2 \left(\Delta y/\sigma^2\right)^2 v^2_Y}{2} = \frac{t^2}{2}\left(\frac{(y_n-y'_n)^2}{\sigma^4}\left(\frac{n}{\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\right)^{-1}\right)\triangleq \frac{t^2\sigma_{Y, Y'}^2}{2}.
\end{align}
Then we conclude that $c_1(\theta)$ is sub-Gaussian with $\sigma^2_{Y, Y'} = \frac{(y_n-y'_n)^2}{\sigma^4}\left(\frac{n}{\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\right)^{-1}$.

To obtain $\bar{\sigma}^{2} = \sup_{(Y,Y'): d(Y, Y')=1}\sigma_{Y,Y'}^2$, we need to assume a bounded data domain, i.e. $\exists\, c\in\mathbb R, k >0$ such that $y\in[c-k\sigma,\,c+k\sigma]$ for every record, then $|y_n-y'_n|\le 2k\sigma$, then
\begin{align}
    \sigma^2_{Y, Y'}\le \frac{(2k\sigma)^2}{\sigma^4}\left(\frac{n}{\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\right)^{-1} = \frac{4k^2}{n+\frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}} = \bar{\sigma}^{2}.\label{eqn:GG_bounded_sigma_bar}
\end{align}
For the reversed pair $(Y',Y)$, $\widetilde c_1(\theta)=-c_1(\theta)$ is Gaussian under $\pi(\theta|Y')$ with variance $(y_n-y'_n)^2v_{Y'}^2/\sigma^4$; since $Y'$ also has $n$ records, $v_{Y'}^2=v_Y^2$, so the same bound holds for $\sigma^2_{Y',Y}$.
Plug Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_bounded_sigma_bar} into Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian},
\begin{align}
\max\{D_{\alpha}(\pi(\theta|Y)\|\pi(\theta|Y')),D_{\alpha}(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))\} \le \frac{\alpha\bar{\sigma}^2}{2}\le \frac{2k^2\alpha}{n+\frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}}.
\end{align}

\bf\raggedright} 
{unbounded neighboring}

\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@

  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@
  \abovedisplayshortskip \z@ \@plus 3\p@
  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Forward direction}Let $Y$ has size $n$ and $Y'$ is of size $n-1$ by removing the $n$-th record $y_n$ in $Y$. Then
\begin{align}
    c_1(\theta)=\log\frac{\ell(\theta;Y)}{\ell(\theta;Y')}
    &=\frac{-(y_n-\theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2) \triangleq \frac{-Z^2}{2\sigma^2}-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2).\label{eqn:GG_unbounded_c1}
\end{align}
where $Z = \theta-y_n$. Under the posterior $\theta|Y \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_Y, v_Y^2)$, then $Z|Y\sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_Y-y_n, v_Y^2)$, and $Z^2/v^2_Y$ follows a noncentral chi-square distribution with degree of freedom 1, that is
\begin{align}
    \frac{Z^2}{v^2_Y}\sim \chi^2_k(\lambda), \quad \text{with }k=1, \lambda = \frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{v^2_Y}.
\end{align}
We note that $c_1(\theta)$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_c1} doesn't fall into the sub-Gaussian regime because $c_1(\theta)$ is a negative quadratic function of a Gaussian random variable, so its exponential moment $\mathbb{E}_P[e^{t c_1(\theta)}]$ is finite only for $|t|$ in a bounded neighborhood of zero (due to the noncentral $\chi^2$ tail of $Z^2$), rather than for all $t\in\mathbb{R}$ as required by the sub-Gaussian condition. So there is no finite $\sigma^2_{Y, Y'}$.

But we can still leverage properties of $\chi^2$ distribution to explicitly calculate each term in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2}. First, the expectation of $Z^2$ is 
\begin{align}
    \mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim \pi(\theta|Y)}\left[Z^2\right] & = v^2_Y(k+\lambda) = v^2_Y\left(1+\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{v^2_Y}\right) = v^2_Y+(\mu_Y-y_n)^2.\label{eqn:GG_unbounded_EZ2}
\end{align}
Then, recall the moment generating function of $\chi^2_k(\lambda)$ is $e^{\lambda t/(1-2t)}{\left(1-2t\right)}^{-k/2}$ for $t<1/2$, hence
\begin{align}
\mathbb E_{\theta\sim \pi(\theta|Y)}\big[e^{tZ^2/v^2_Y}\big]
&=\exp\left({\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{v^2_Y}\cdot\frac{t}{1-2t}}\right){\left(1-2t\right)}^{-1/2}\notag\\
\Rightarrow\; \mathbb E_{\theta\sim \pi(\theta|Y)}\big[e^{tZ^2}\big]
&=\exp\left({\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{v^2_Y}\cdot\frac{v^2_Yt}{1-2v^2_Yt}}\right){\left(1-2tv^2_Y\right)}^{-1/2}\notag\\
&=(1-2v_Y^2 t)^{-1/2}
\exp\left(\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2t}{1-2v_Y^2 t}\right),
\qquad t<\frac{1}{2v_Y^2}.\label{eqn:GG_unbounded_mgf_Z2}
\end{align}
Now we have two approaches to explicitly upper bound $D_{\alpha}(\pi(\theta|Y)\|\pi(\theta|Y'))$. 

\noindent 1) Derive sub-exponential parameters $b_Y, \nu_Y$ for $c_1(\theta)$ and apply Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential}. Since the derivation of $b_Y, \nu_Y$ is not tight, the resulting RDP bound is generally not tight; 

\noindent 2) Directly calculate each term in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape} using the closed-form moment generating function in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_mgf_Z2}. This approach yields an exact expression.

\textbf{Method 1: sub-exponential parameters.\footnote{For brevity, we write $b_Y,\nu_Y$ for $b_{Y,Y'},\nu_{Y,Y'}$, and only the forward direction is illustrated here.}}
Our target is to identify $b_Y, \nu^2_Y$ such that
\begin{align}
    \log\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim P}\left(e^{t(c_1(\theta)-\mathbb E_P\left[c_1(\theta)\right])}\right)\le \frac{t^2\nu^2_{Y}}{2(1-b_{Y}|t|)}
\end{align}
for $|t| < 1/b_Y$, with $c_1(\theta)$ defined in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_c1}.
\begin{align}
    c_1(\theta)-\mathbb E_P\left[c_1(\theta)\right] & = \frac{-Z^2}{2\sigma^2}-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2) - \mathbb E_P\left(\frac{-Z^2}{2\sigma^2}-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)\right)\notag\\
    & = -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left(Z^2-\mathbb E_P(Z^2)\right)
\end{align}
So we only need to examine $(Z^2-\mathbb E_P(Z^2))$:
\begin{align}
    &\log \mathbb{E}_P\left[e^{t(Z^2-\mathbb E_P(Z^2))}\right]\notag\\
    =\; & \log \mathbb{E}_P\left[e^{t(Z^2-v^2_Y-(\mu_Y-y_n)^2)}\right]\qquad \text{ per Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_EZ2}}\notag\\
    =\; & \log \mathbb{E}_P\left[e^{tZ^2}\right]-\left(v^2_Y+(\mu_Y-y_n)^2\right)t \notag\\
    =\; & -\frac{1}{2}\log(1-2v_Y^2 t)-v^2_Y t+(\mu_Y-y_n)^2t\left(\frac{1}{1-2v_Y^2 t}-1\right)\qquad \text{ per Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_mgf_Z2}}\notag\\
    =\; &  \frac{1}{2}\left[-\log(1-x)-x\right]+(\mu_Y-y_n)^2t\left(\frac{1}{1-x}-1\right) \qquad \text{let $x = 2v_Y^2t$}.\label{eqn:GG_unbounded_subexp}
\end{align}
Since Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_mgf_Z2} requires $t<1/(2v_Y^2)$,  $x = 2v_Y^2t < 1$. To ensure Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_subexp}$\le \frac{t^2\nu'^2_{Y}}{2(1-b'_{Y}|t|)}$ for some $b'_Y, \nu'_Y$ when $|t|<1/b'_Y$, we need some inequalities below for $|x|<1$
\begin{align}
-\log(1-x)-x &\le \frac{x^2}{2(1-|x|)}, \qquad 
\frac{1}{1-x}-1  = \frac{x}{1-x} \le \frac{|x|}{1-|x|}.
\end{align}
Plug the inequalities above into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_subexp}:
\begin{align}
    \text{Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_subexp}}& \le \frac{1}{2}\cdot \frac{x^2}{2(1-|x|)} + (\mu_Y-y_n)^2|t|\cdot \frac{|x|}{1-|x|}\notag\\
    & = \frac{1}{2}\cdot \frac{4v_Y^4t^2}{2(1-2v_Y^2|t|)} + (\mu_Y-y_n)^2|t|\cdot \frac{2v_Y^2|t|}{1-2v_Y^2|t|}\notag\\
    & = \frac{v_Y^4t^2}{1-2v_Y^2|t|} + \frac{2v_Y^2(\mu_Y-y_n)^2t^2}{1-2v_Y^2|t|}\notag\\
    &= \frac{2\left(v_Y^2+2(\mu_Y-y_n)^2\right)v_Y^2t^2}{2(1-2v_Y^2|t|)}\triangleq  \frac{t^2\nu'^2_{Y}}{2(1-b'_{Y}|t|)}.
\end{align}
Therefore $b'_Y = 2v_Y^2$ and $\nu'^2_Y=2\left(v_Y^2+2(\mu_Y-y_n)^2\right)v_Y^2$. 

Recall that $b'_Y, \nu'^2_Y$ are for $(Z^2-\mathbb E_P(Z^2))$, while $c_1(\theta)-\mathbb E_P\left[c_1(\theta)\right]  = -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left(Z^2-\mathbb E_P(Z^2)\right)$, hence $b_Y = b'_Y/2\sigma^2$ and $\nu^2_Y = \nu'^2_Y/4\sigma^4$. Plug in Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential}:
\begin{align}
D_{\alpha}(P||Q)  &\le \frac{\nu_{Y}^2}{2}
    \frac{\alpha(1-2b_{Y})+2b_{Y}}{(1-b_{Y})\bigl(1-b_{Y}(\alpha-1)\bigr)}\notag\\
    & = \frac{ v_Y^2\bigl(v_Y^2+2(\mu_Y-y_n)^2\bigr)}{4\sigma^4}\frac{\alpha\!\left(1-\tfrac{2v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}\right)+\tfrac{2v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}}
    {\left(1-\tfrac{v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}\right)\left(1-\tfrac{v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}(\alpha-1)\right)}\notag\\
    & = \frac{ v_Y^2\bigl(v_Y^2+2(\mu_Y-y_n)^2\bigr)}{4\sigma^2}\cdot\frac{\alpha\sigma^2-2v_Y^2(\alpha-1)}
    {\left(\sigma^2-v^2_Y\right)\left(\sigma^2-v_Y^2(\alpha-1)\right)},
\end{align}
where $v^2_Y$ and $\mu_Y$ are defined in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_post_para}.

\textbf{Method 2: exact moment calculation}
For the first term in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}, per the $c_1(\theta)$ defined in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_c1}, and the moment generating function of $Z^2$ derived in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_mgf_Z2}
\begin{align}
-c_1(\theta)&=\frac{Z^2}{2\sigma^2}+\frac12\log(2\pi\sigma^2)\notag\\
\mathbb E_P[e^{-c_1(\theta)}] & = \mathbb E_P\left[\exp\left(\frac{Z^2}{2\sigma^2}+\frac12\log(2\pi\sigma^2)\right)\right]\notag\\
&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{\frac12}\mathbb E_P\left[\exp\left(\frac{Z^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)\right] \notag\\
&= (2\pi\sigma^2)^{\frac12}\left(1-2v_Y^2 \frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\right)^{-\frac12}
\exp\left(\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}}{1-2v_Y^2 \frac{1}{2\sigma^2}}\right)\text{ plug $t=\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_mgf_Z2}}\notag\\
& = (2\pi\sigma^2)^{\frac12}\left(1- \frac{v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}\right)^{-\frac12}\exp\left(\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2 -v^2_Y)}\right).
\end{align}
This requires $t=\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}<\frac{1}{2v_Y^2}$, equivalently $v_Y^2<\sigma^2$, recall $v^2_Y=\left(\frac{n}{\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\right)^{-1}$, this holds for $n\ge1$ in the Gaussian-Gaussian conjugate model. Then  per Lemma~\ref{lemma:c2_in_c1}
\begin{align}
    c_2 &= \log \mathbb E_P[e^{-c_1(\theta)}] = \frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1- \frac{v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}\right)+\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2 -v^2_Y)}. \label{eqn:GG_unbounded_1st}
\end{align}
Next, for the second term in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}, we also use the moment generating function for $Z^2$ in  Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_mgf_Z2} to exactly calculate the ($\alpha-1$) moment for $c_1(\theta)$:
\begin{align}
(\alpha-1)c_1(\theta)
&= -\frac{\alpha-1}{2\sigma^2}Z^2-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2),\notag\\
\mathbb E_P[e^{(\alpha-1)c_1(\theta)}]  & = \mathbb E_P\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha-1}{2\sigma^2}Z^2-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)\right)\right]\notag\\
&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\mathbb E_P\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha-1}{2\sigma^2}Z^2\right)\right] \text{ plug $t=\frac{-(\alpha-1)}{2\sigma^2}$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_mgf_Z2}}\notag\\
& = (2\pi\sigma^2)^{-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\left(1-2v_Y^2 \left(\frac{1-\alpha}{2\sigma^2}\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
\exp\left(\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2\left(\frac{1-\alpha}{2\sigma^2}\right)}{1-2v_Y^2 \left(\frac{1-\alpha}{2\sigma^2}\right)}\right)\notag\\
& = (2\pi\sigma^2)^{-\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\left(1+v_Y^2 \left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\sigma^2}\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
\exp\left(\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2(1-\alpha)}{2(\sigma^2+(\alpha-1)v_Y^2)}\right).
\end{align}
This requires $t=\frac{-(\alpha-1)}{2\sigma^2} < \frac{1}{2v^2_Y}$. Since $\alpha>1, \frac{-(\alpha-1)}{2\sigma^2} < 0$, so this requirement always holds.
Next, the 2nd term in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape} can be calculated as
\begin{align}
    &\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\mathbb E_P\left[e^{(\alpha-1)c_1(\theta)}\right]\notag\\
    =\;&-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\left(1+v_Y^2 \left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\sigma^2}\right)\right)-\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2+(\alpha-1)v_Y^2)}.\label{eqn:GG_unbounded_2nd}
\end{align}
Plug $c_2$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_1st} and $\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{P}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)\cdot c_1(\theta)}\right]$ in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_2nd} into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}, we have
\begin{align}
    &D_{\alpha}(\pi(\theta|Y)||\pi(\theta|Y')) \notag\\
    =\; & c_2 +\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log \mathbb{E}_{P}\left[e^{(\alpha-1)\cdot c_1(\theta)}\right]\notag\\
    =\;&\cancel{\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)}-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1- \frac{v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}\right)+\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2 -v^2_Y)} \notag\\
    &\cancel{-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)}-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\left(1+v_Y^2 \left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\sigma^2}\right)\right)-\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2+(\alpha-1)v_Y^2)}\notag\\
    =\;&-\frac{1}{2}\log\!\left(\!1- \frac{v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}\right)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha\!-\!1)}\log\!\left(\!1+v_Y^2 \left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\sigma^2}\right)\right)+\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2 -v^2_Y)}-\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2+(\alpha-1)v_Y^2)}.\notag
\end{align}
Recall
\begin{align}
v^2_Y=\left(\frac{n}{\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\right)^{-1},
\qquad
\mu_Y=v^2_Y\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n y_i+\frac{\mu_0}{\tau^2}\right).
\end{align}
Then define $\omega = n + \frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}$ and assume $y_n \in [\mu_Y-k^*\sigma, \mu_Y + k^*\sigma]$.
\begin{align}
    &D_{\alpha}(\pi(\theta|Y)||\pi(\theta|Y')) \notag\\
    =\;&-\frac{1}{2}\log\!\left(\!1- \frac{v_Y^2}{\sigma^2}\right)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha\!-\!1)}\log\!\left(\!1+v_Y^2 \left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\sigma^2}\right)\right)+\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2 -v^2_Y)}-\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2+(\alpha-1)v_Y^2)}\notag\\
    =\; & -\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1- \omega^{-1}\right)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha\!-\!1)}\log\!\left(\!1+\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega}\right) +\frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2}\left[\frac{1}{1-\omega^{-1}}-\frac{1}{1+\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega}}\right]\notag\\
    \triangleq\; & A(\alpha, \omega) + \frac{(\mu_Y-y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2} B(\alpha, \omega)\notag\\
    \le\; & A(\alpha, \omega)+\frac{k^{*2}}{2}B(\alpha, \omega)\label{eqn:eps_GG_unbounded_ape}
\end{align}
Since $\mu_Y=\big(\frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}\mu_0+\sum_{i=1}^n y_i\big)/\omega$ is a convex combination of $\mu_0$ and $\{y_i\}$, if all records and $\mu_0$ lie in $[c-M,c+M]$, then $|\mu_Y-y_n|\le 2M$, i.e., $k^*=2M/\sigma$ is a data-independent choice.

\begin{claim}\label{claim:mono}
For all $\alpha>1$ and $\omega>1$, we have $A(\alpha,\omega)>0$ and $B(\alpha,\omega)>0$. Furthermore, holding $\alpha$ fixed, both $A(\alpha,\omega)$ and $B(\alpha,\omega)$ decrease monotonically as $\omega$ increases.
\end{claim}
\begin{proof}
    Assume $n \ge 1$, then $\omega = n + \sigma^2/\tau^2 > 1$. Let $x = \omega^{-1}$ and $t = \alpha-1$, then $x\in (0,1)$ and $t>0$, and
    \begin{align}
        A(\alpha, \omega) = -\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1- x\right)-\frac{1}{2t}\log\left(1+xt\right) \triangleq A(x).
    \end{align}
    Thus to prove $A(\alpha, \omega)>0$ is equivalent to prove 
    \begin{align}
        -\log\left(1- x\right)>\frac{1}{t}\log\left(1+xt\right).
    \end{align}
    Since $-\log(1-x) > x$ for $x\in (0,1)$, and $\log(1+xt) < xt\Rightarrow t^{-1}\log(1+xt) < x$,
    \begin{align}
        -\log\left(1- x\right)>x > \frac{1}{t}\log\left(1+xt\right),
    \end{align}
    which completes the proof of strictly positivity of $A(\alpha, \omega)$. Then, take derivative of $x$ on $A(x)$:
    \begin{align}
        A'(x) &= \frac{1}{2(1-x)} - \frac{1}{2(1+xt)} > 0,
    \end{align}
    per $x\in (0,1)$ and $t > 0$. Thus,
    \begin{align}
        \frac{\partial A}{\partial \omega} = A'(x)\frac{d x}{d \omega} = -\omega^2 A'(x) < 0.
    \end{align}
    Furthermore, take derivative of $x$ on $B(x) = \frac{1}{1-x}-\frac{1}{1+xt}$
    \begin{align}
        B'(x) = \frac{1}{(1-x)^2} + \frac{t}{(1+xt)^2} > 0 \quad \Rightarrow \frac{\partial B}{\partial \omega} < 0
    \end{align}
    thus, both $A(\alpha,\omega)$ and $B(\alpha,\omega)$ decrease monotonically as $\omega$ increases.
\end{proof}

\bf} 
{Reverse direction}
We next analyze the reverse R\'enyi divergence $D_\alpha(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))$, where $Y$ has size $n$ and $Y'$ is of size $n-1$, obtained by removing the $n$-th record $y_n$ from $Y$. Define
\begin{align}
\widetilde c_1(\theta)&\triangleq\log\frac{\ell(\theta;Y')}{\ell(\theta;Y)}=-\log p(y_n|\theta)=\frac{(y_n-\theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)\triangleq\frac{Z'^2}{2\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)\notag\\
&=-c_1(\theta),\label{eqn:c1_GG_reverse}
\end{align}
where $Z'=\theta-y_n$. Under the posterior $\theta|Y'\sim\mathcal N(\mu_{Y'},v_{Y'}^2)$, $Z'|Y'\sim\mathcal N(\mu_{Y'}-y_n,v_{Y'}^2)$, and $Z'^2/v_{Y'}^2$ follows a noncentral chi-square distribution with one degree of freedom:
\begin{align}
\frac{Z'^2}{v_{Y'}^2}\sim\chi_1^2(\lambda'),\qquad\lambda'=\frac{(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2}{v_{Y'}^2}.\label{eqn:noncentral_chisq_reverse}
\end{align}
Recall that the MGF of $\chi_k^2(\lambda)$ is $\exp\big(\frac{\lambda t}{1-2t}\big)(1-2t)^{-k/2}$ for $t<\frac{1}{2}$. Therefore,
\begin{align}
\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi(\theta|Y')}\big[e^{tZ'^2}\big]=\left(1-2v_{Y'}^2t\right)^{-1/2}\exp\left(\frac{(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2t}{1-2v_{Y'}^2t}\right),\qquad t<\frac{1}{2v_{Y'}^2}.\label{eqn:MGF_Z2_reverse}
\end{align}
We now directly calculate the two terms in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape} using the closed-form MGF in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:MGF_Z2_reverse}. For the first term, per Eq.~\eqref{eqn:c1_GG_reverse}, $-\widetilde c_1(\theta)=-\frac{Z'^2}{2\sigma^2}-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)$, and hence
\begin{align}
\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi(\theta|Y')}\big[e^{-\widetilde c_1(\theta)}\big]&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-1/2}\,\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi(\theta|Y')}\Big[\exp\Big(-\frac{Z'^2}{2\sigma^2}\Big)\Big]\notag\\
&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-1/2}\Big(1+\frac{v_{Y'}^2}{\sigma^2}\Big)^{-1/2}\exp\Big(-\frac{(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2+v_{Y'}^2)}\Big),\label{eqn:first_moment_reverse}
\end{align}
where we plug $t=-1/(2\sigma^2)$ into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:MGF_Z2_reverse}. Since $t<0$, the MGF condition always holds. Therefore, per Lemma~\ref{lemma:c2_in_c1},
\begin{align}
\widetilde c_2=\log\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi(\theta|Y')}\big[e^{-\widetilde c_1(\theta)}\big]=-\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)-\frac{1}{2}\log\Big(1+\frac{v_{Y'}^2}{\sigma^2}\Big)-\frac{(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2+v_{Y'}^2)}.\label{eqn:c2_reverse}
\end{align}
Next, for the second term in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}, $(\alpha-1)\widetilde c_1(\theta)=\frac{\alpha-1}{2\sigma^2}Z'^2+\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)$. Thus,
\begin{align}
\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi(\theta|Y')}\big[e^{(\alpha-1)\widetilde c_1(\theta)}\big]&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{(\alpha-1)/2}\,\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi(\theta|Y')}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{\alpha-1}{2\sigma^2}Z'^2\Big)\Big]\notag\\
&=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{(\alpha-1)/2}\Big(1-\frac{(\alpha-1)v_{Y'}^2}{\sigma^2}\Big)^{-1/2}\exp\Big(\frac{(\alpha-1)(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2-(\alpha-1)v_{Y'}^2)}\Big),\label{eqn:second_moment_reverse}
\end{align}
where we plug $t=\frac{\alpha-1}{2\sigma^2}$ into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:MGF_Z2_reverse}. This requires
\begin{align}
\frac{\alpha-1}{2\sigma^2}<\frac{1}{2v_{Y'}^2},\quad\text{or equivalently}\quad(\alpha-1)v_{Y'}^2<\sigma^2.\label{eqn:reverse_finiteness_v}
\end{align}
Consequently,
\begin{align}
\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi(\theta|Y')}\big[e^{(\alpha-1)\widetilde c_1(\theta)}\big]=&\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\Big(1-\frac{(\alpha-1)v_{Y'}^2}{\sigma^2}\Big)\notag\\
&+\frac{(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2-(\alpha-1)v_{Y'}^2)}.\label{eqn:second_term_reverse}
\end{align}
Plugging Eqs.~\eqref{eqn:c2_reverse} and \eqref{eqn:second_term_reverse} into Eq.~\eqref{eqn:D_alpha_general_c2_ape}, the $\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2)$ terms cancel, and we obtain
\begin{align}
D_\alpha(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))=&-\frac{1}{2}\log\Big(1+\frac{v_{Y'}^2}{\sigma^2}\Big)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\Big(1-\frac{(\alpha-1)v_{Y'}^2}{\sigma^2}\Big)\notag\\
&+\frac{(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2-(\alpha-1)v_{Y'}^2)}-\frac{(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2}{2(\sigma^2+v_{Y'}^2)}.\label{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_v}
\end{align}
Recall that $v_{Y'}^2=\big(\frac{n-1}{\sigma^2}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\big)^{-1}=\frac{\sigma^2}{\omega-1}$ with $\omega=n+\frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}$. Then the finiteness condition in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:reverse_finiteness_v} becomes
\begin{align}
\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega-1}<1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad1<\alpha<\omega.\label{eqn:reverse_finiteness_omega}
\end{align}
For $1<\alpha<\omega$, Eq.~\eqref{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_v} becomes
\begin{align}
D_\alpha(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))=&-\frac{1}{2}\log\Big(\frac{\omega}{\omega-1}\Big)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\Big(\frac{\omega-\alpha}{\omega-1}\Big)\notag\\
&+\frac{(\mu_{Y'}-y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2}\Big[\frac{1}{1-\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega-1}}-\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\omega-1}}\Big].\label{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_mup}
\end{align}
The posterior means satisfy
\begin{align}
\omega\mu_{Y}=(\omega-1)\mu_{Y'}+y_n\;\Rightarrow\;\mu_{Y'}-y_n=\frac{\omega}{\omega-1}(\mu_{Y}-y_n).\label{eqn:posterior_mean_reverse_relation}
\end{align}
Therefore, for $1<\alpha<\omega$,
\begin{align}
D_\alpha(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))&=-\frac{1}{2}\log\Big(\frac{\omega}{\omega-1}\Big)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\Big(\frac{\omega-\alpha}{\omega-1}\Big)+\frac{(\mu_{Y}-y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2}\frac{\alpha\omega}{(\omega-1)(\omega-\alpha)}\notag\\
&\triangleq\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)+\frac{(\mu_{Y}-y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2}\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega),\label{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_final}
\end{align}
where
\begin{align}
&\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)\triangleq-\frac{1}{2}\log\Big(\frac{\omega}{\omega-1}\Big)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\Big(\frac{\omega-\alpha}{\omega-1}\Big),\notag\\
&\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega)\triangleq\frac{\alpha\omega}{(\omega-1)(\omega-\alpha)}.
\end{align}
For comparison, the corresponding quantities for the forward direction in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_GG_unbounded_ape} are
\begin{align}
&A(\alpha,\omega)=-\frac{1}{2}\log\Big(\frac{\omega-1}{\omega}\Big)-\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}\log\Big(1+\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega}\Big),\notag\\
& B(\alpha,\omega)=\frac{\alpha\omega}{(\omega-1)(\omega+\alpha-1)}.\notag
\end{align}
If $|\mu_{Y}-y_n|\leq k^*\sigma$, then for every $1<\alpha<\omega$,
\begin{align}
D_\alpha(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))\leq\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)+\frac{k^{*2}}{2}\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega).\label{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_upper}
\end{align}
For $\alpha\geq\omega$, the MGF in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:second_moment_reverse} is not finite, and therefore
\begin{align}
D_\alpha(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))=\infty,\quad\alpha\geq\omega;\qquad\text{in particular, }D_\infty(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))=\infty.\label{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_infinite}
\end{align}

\begin{claim}\label{claim:reverse-AB}
For all $1<\alpha<\omega$, $\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)>0$ and $\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega)>0$. Holding $\alpha$ fixed, both decrease monotonically in $\omega$ on $(\alpha,\infty)$, and $\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)\to\infty$ as $\omega\downarrow\alpha$.
\end{claim}
\begin{proof}
Let $u=(\omega-1)^{-1}$ and $t=\alpha-1>0$. The condition $1<\alpha<\omega$ is equivalent to $0<tu<1$, and $\omega/(\omega-1)=1+u$, $(\omega-\alpha)/(\omega-1)=1-tu$. Hence $\widetilde A=-\frac12\log(1+u)-\frac{1}{2t}\log(1-tu)$ and $\widetilde B=\frac{\alpha u(1+u)}{1-tu}$. Since $-\log(1-y)>y$ for $y\in(0,1)$ and $\log(1+u)<u$ for $u>0$, we have $-\frac1t\log(1-tu)>u>\log(1+u)$, so $\widetilde A>0$; $\widetilde B>0$ is immediate. Further, $\frac{\partial\widetilde A}{\partial u}=\frac12\big(\frac{1}{1-tu}-\frac{1}{1+u}\big)>0$, and $\widetilde B$ is increasing in $u$ because its numerator increases and its positive denominator decreases in $u$. Since $u$ is decreasing in $\omega$, both $\widetilde A$ and $\widetilde B$ decrease in $\omega$. Finally, $tu\uparrow1$ as $\omega\downarrow\alpha$, so $-\log(1-tu)\to\infty$.
\end{proof}

\bf} 
{Two-sided guarantee.}
Combining Eqs.~\eqref{eqn:eps_GG_unbounded_ape} and \eqref{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_upper}, if $|\mu_Y-y_n|\le k^*\sigma$, then for every $1<\alpha<\omega$,
\begin{align}
&\max\{D_\alpha(\pi(\theta|Y)\|\pi(\theta|Y')),D_\alpha(\pi(\theta|Y')\|\pi(\theta|Y))\}\notag\\
\le\;&\max\Big\{A(\alpha,\omega)+\frac{k^{*2}}{2}B(\alpha,\omega),\ \widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)+\frac{k^{*2}}{2}\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega)\Big\},
\end{align}
so OPS satisfies RDP of this order only for $1<\alpha<\omega$, and does not satisfy pure DP under unbounded neighboring. Note that $\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega)>B(\alpha,\omega)$ since $\omega-\alpha<\omega+\alpha-1$. As $\omega=n+\sigma^2/\tau^2$ depends on $n$, a guarantee holding uniformly over all $n\ge1$ is obtained by evaluating at $\omega_{\min}=1+\sigma^2/\tau^2$, and is finite only for $1<\alpha<1+\sigma^2/\tau^2$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- claim-3: body_md -->
\label{claim:mono}
For all $\alpha>1$ and $\omega>1$, we have $A(\alpha,\omega)>0$ and $B(\alpha,\omega)>0$. Furthermore, holding $\alpha$ fixed, both $A(\alpha,\omega)$ and $B(\alpha,\omega)$ decrease monotonically as $\omega$ increases.
<!-- end claim-3 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Assume $n \ge 1$, then $\omega = n + \sigma^2/\tau^2 > 1$. Let $x = \omega^{-1}$ and $t = \alpha-1$, then $x\in (0,1)$ and $t>0$, and
    \begin{align}
        A(\alpha, \omega) = -\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1- x\right)-\frac{1}{2t}\log\left(1+xt\right) \triangleq A(x).
    \end{align}
    Thus to prove $A(\alpha, \omega)>0$ is equivalent to prove 
    \begin{align}
        -\log\left(1- x\right)>\frac{1}{t}\log\left(1+xt\right).
    \end{align}
    Since $-\log(1-x) > x$ for $x\in (0,1)$, and $\log(1+xt) < xt\Rightarrow t^{-1}\log(1+xt) < x$,
    \begin{align}
        -\log\left(1- x\right)>x > \frac{1}{t}\log\left(1+xt\right),
    \end{align}
    which completes the proof of strictly positivity of $A(\alpha, \omega)$. Then, take derivative of $x$ on $A(x)$:
    \begin{align}
        A'(x) &= \frac{1}{2(1-x)} - \frac{1}{2(1+xt)} > 0,
    \end{align}
    per $x\in (0,1)$ and $t > 0$. Thus,
    \begin{align}
        \frac{\partial A}{\partial \omega} = A'(x)\frac{d x}{d \omega} = -\omega^2 A'(x) < 0.
    \end{align}
    Furthermore, take derivative of $x$ on $B(x) = \frac{1}{1-x}-\frac{1}{1+xt}$
    \begin{align}
        B'(x) = \frac{1}{(1-x)^2} + \frac{t}{(1+xt)^2} > 0 \quad \Rightarrow \frac{\partial B}{\partial \omega} < 0
    \end{align}
    thus, both $A(\alpha,\omega)$ and $B(\alpha,\omega)$ decrease monotonically as $\omega$ increases.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- claim-4: body_md -->
\label{claim:reverse-AB}
For all $1<\alpha<\omega$, $\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)>0$ and $\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega)>0$. Holding $\alpha$ fixed, both decrease monotonically in $\omega$ on $(\alpha,\infty)$, and $\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega)\to\infty$ as $\omega\downarrow\alpha$.
<!-- end claim-4 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Let $u=(\omega-1)^{-1}$ and $t=\alpha-1>0$. The condition $1<\alpha<\omega$ is equivalent to $0<tu<1$, and $\omega/(\omega-1)=1+u$, $(\omega-\alpha)/(\omega-1)=1-tu$. Hence $\widetilde A=-\frac12\log(1+u)-\frac{1}{2t}\log(1-tu)$ and $\widetilde B=\frac{\alpha u(1+u)}{1-tu}$. Since $-\log(1-y)>y$ for $y\in(0,1)$ and $\log(1+u)<u$ for $u>0$, we have $-\frac1t\log(1-tu)>u>\log(1+u)$, so $\widetilde A>0$; $\widetilde B>0$ is immediate. Further, $\frac{\partial\widetilde A}{\partial u}=\frac12\big(\frac{1}{1-tu}-\frac{1}{1+u}\big)>0$, and $\widetilde B$ is increasing in $u$ because its numerator increases and its positive denominator decreases in $u$. Since $u$ is decreasing in $\omega$, both $\widetilde A$ and $\widetilde B$ decrease in $\omega$. Finally, $tu\uparrow1$ as $\omega\downarrow\alpha$, so $-\log(1-tu)\to\infty$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Let \(\mathbf{x}_i \in \mathbb{R}^d\) be covariates and $y_i \in \mathbb{R}$ be response for the $i$-th individual. Given the dataset \(\mathbf{Y}= \{(\mathbf{x}_i,y_i)\}_{i=1}^n\),  let $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$ be the design matrix, and $\mathbf{y}\in \mathbb{R}^n$ be the response vector. The response follows a Gaussian distribution: $y_i|\mathbf{x}_i,\boldsymbol\beta \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta,\sigma^2)$. Impose a Gaussian prior on regression coefficient $\boldsymbol\beta$, i.e. $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta)= \mathcal N(0,\tau^2 \mathbf{I}_d)$, then the posterior is $\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y} = \mathcal N(\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y},\Sigma_\mathbf{Y})$, where $\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}=\Big(\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\mathbf{I}_d\Big)^{-1}$, and $\boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathbf{Y} = \Sigma_\mathbf{Y} \left( \frac{1}{\sigma^2} \mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{y} \right)$.

\bf\raggedright} 
{bounded neighboring}\label{ape:LR_bounded}
Let $\mathbf{Y}$ and $\mathbf{Y}'$ differ in exactly one record, i.e. $\mathbf{z}_n=(\mathbf{x}_n,y_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}$ is replaced by $\mathbf{z}'_n=(\mathbf{x}'_n,y'_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}'$, then
\begin{align}
c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=\log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y})}{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y}')}
    &=\log p(y_n|\mathbf{x}_n,\boldsymbol\beta) - \log p(y'_n|\mathbf{x}'_n,\boldsymbol\beta)\notag\\
    & = -\frac{(y_n-\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)^2-(y_n'-\mathbf{x}_n'^\top\boldsymbol\beta)^2}{2\sigma^2}.
\end{align}

\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@

  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Case 1:} If $\mathbf{x}_n = \mathbf{x}_n'$, then
\begin{align}
    c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)
    & = -\frac{(y_n^2-y_n'^2)-2(y_n-y_n')\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta}{2\sigma^2}.
\end{align}
Since $\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y} = \mathcal N(\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y},\Sigma_\mathbf{Y})$ and per the linear transformation of Gaussian random variable, we have $\mathbf{x}_n^{\top}\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{x}_n^{\top}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{x}_n^{\top}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} \mathbf{x}_n)$. Therefore, $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ under $P = \pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})$ is Gaussian with variance
\begin{align}
    \sigma_{\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'}^2 = \mbox{Var}_{\boldsymbol\beta\sim\pi(\cdot|\mathbf{Y})}[c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)]= \frac{(y_n-y_n')^2}{\sigma^4}\,\mathbf{x}_n^\top\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{x}_n.
\end{align}
Since $\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{x}$ is positive semi-definite, $\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}=\Big(\frac{1}{\sigma^2}\mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}+\frac{1}{\tau^2}\mathbf{I}_d\Big)^{-1}\preceq
\left(\frac{1}{\tau^2} \mathbf{I}_d\right)^{-1} = \tau^2 \mathbf{I}_d$. If additionally assume $|y_n-y_n'|\le 2k\sigma$, then
\begin{align}
\sigma_{\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{Y}'}^2 &= \frac{(y_n-y_n')^2}{\sigma^4}\,\mathbf{x}_n^\top\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{x}_n \notag\\
&\le\frac{4k^2\sigma^2}{\sigma^4}\mathbf{x}_n^\top(\tau^2\mathbf{I}_d)\mathbf{x}_n = \frac{4k^2}{\sigma^2}\tau^2\|\mathbf{x}_n\|^2 \le \frac{4k^2\tau^2R^2}{\sigma^2} \triangleq \bar{\sigma}^2.
\end{align}
For the reversed pair $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$, $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=-c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is Gaussian under $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')$ with variance $(y_n-y'_n)^2\mathbf{x}_n^\top\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}\mathbf{x}_n/\sigma^4$; since $\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}\preceq\tau^2\mathbf I_d$, the same bound holds for $\sigma^2_{\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y}}$.
Per Corollary~\ref{coro:subgaussian},
\begin{align}
\max\{D_\alpha(P\|Q),D_\alpha(Q\|P)\}\le \frac{\alpha\bar\sigma^2}{2}\le\frac{2\alpha k^2\tau^2 R^2}{\sigma^2}.
\end{align}

\@xpt\@xipt
  \abovedisplayskip      7\p@ \@plus 2\p@ \@minus 5\p@

  \belowdisplayskip      \abovedisplayskip
  \belowdisplayshortskip 4\p@ \@plus 3\p@ \@minus 3\p@
\bf} 
{Case 2: } 
When $\mathbf{x}_n \neq \mathbf{x}_n'$, the log-likelihood ratio decomposes as 
$c_1(\boldsymbol\beta) = L(\boldsymbol\beta) + Q(\boldsymbol\beta)$, where
\begin{align}
L(\boldsymbol\beta) &= -\frac{(y_n^2-y_n'^2) - 
2(y_n\mathbf{x}_n-y_n'\mathbf{x}_n')^\top\boldsymbol\beta}{2\sigma^2}, \\
Q(\boldsymbol\beta) &= -\frac{\boldsymbol\beta^\top A\boldsymbol\beta}{2\sigma^2}, \quad 
A \triangleq \mathbf{x}_n\mathbf{x}_n^\top - \mathbf{x}_n'\mathbf{x}_n'^\top.
\end{align}
Since $A$ may be indefinite, $Q(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is a linear combination 
of non-central $\chi^2$ random variables with potentially negative 
coefficients under $\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y} \sim \mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}, 
\Sigma_\mathbf{Y})$, so $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is generally not sub-Gaussian. 
We show that it satisfies a sub-exponential MGF bound under 
additional boundedness assumptions, by bounding the MGF of each 
part separately and combining via Cauchy-Schwarz. Let $\mu_L = 
\mathbb{E}[L(\boldsymbol\beta)]$, $\mu_Q = \mathbb{E}[Q(\boldsymbol\beta)]$, 
and $\mu^{c_1}_\mathbf{Y} = \mu_L + \mu_Q$\footnote{For brevity, we write $\mu^{c_1}_\mathbf{Y},\nu^2_\mathbf{Y},b_\mathbf{Y}$ for $\mu_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'},\nu^2_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'},b_{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}'}$ in Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential}.}.

\textbf{Step 1: MGF of the linear part.}
Since $L(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is affine in $\boldsymbol\beta$ and $\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y} 
\sim \mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y},\Sigma_\mathbf{Y})$, $L(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is 
Gaussian under the posterior. Using $\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} \preceq 
\tau^2\mathbf{I}_d$, $|y_i| \leq M_y$, and $\|\mathbf{x}_i\| \leq R$:
\begin{align}
\mathrm{Var}[L(\boldsymbol\beta)] 
= \frac{(y_n\mathbf{x}_n-y_n'\mathbf{x}_n')^\top\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}
(y_n\mathbf{x}_n-y_n'\mathbf{x}_n')}{\sigma^4} 
&\leq \frac{\|y_n\mathbf{x}_n-y_n'\mathbf{x}_n'\|^2\tau^2}{\sigma^4} \notag\\
&\leq \frac{4M_y^2R^2\tau^2}{\sigma^4} 
\triangleq \bar{\sigma}^2_L.
\end{align}
Therefore $L(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is sub-Gaussian with variance proxy 
$\bar{\sigma}^2_L$ and for all $t \in \mathbb{R}$:
\begin{align}
\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{t(L(\boldsymbol\beta)-\mu_L)}\right] 
\leq \frac{t^2\bar{\sigma}^2_L}{2}.
\label{eqn:mgf_L_case2}
\end{align}

\textbf{Step 2: Centered MGF of the quadratic part.}
We compute $\mu_Q$ exactly. For $\boldsymbol\beta \sim 
\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y},\Sigma_\mathbf{Y})$:
\begin{align}
\mu_Q = -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol\beta^\top A\boldsymbol\beta] 
= -\frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\left(\mathrm{tr}(A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}) + 
\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\right).
\label{eqn:mu_Q_case2}
\end{align}
Define the symmetrized matrix $\tilde{A} \triangleq 
\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}^{1/2}A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}^{1/2}$. By Sylvester's determinant 
identity:
\begin{align}
\det\!\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A\right) 
= \det\!\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}
\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}^{1/2}A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}^{1/2}\right) 
= \det\!\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\tilde{A}\right),
\end{align}
so the positivity condition $\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}
\tilde{A} \succ 0$ controls both the determinant and the 
existence of the MGF, while the inverse term is retained in 
the nonsymmetric form $(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}
\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A)^{-1}$, which is algebraically equivalent. 
Since $\tilde{A}$ is symmetric:
\begin{align}
\|\tilde{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}} = 
\|\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}^{1/2}A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}} 
\leq \|\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}} 
\leq \tau^2(\|\mathbf{x}_n\|^2+\|\mathbf{x}_n'\|^2) \leq 2\tau^2R^2,
\end{align}
so the positivity condition holds for $|t| < b_Q^{-1}$ where 
$b_Q \triangleq 2\tau^2R^2/\sigma^2$. The raw MGF of 
$Q(\boldsymbol\beta)$ is:
\begin{align}
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{tQ(\boldsymbol\beta)}\right] 
= \frac{\exp\!\left(-\dfrac{t}{2\sigma^2}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A
\left(\mathbf{I}+\dfrac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A\right)^{-1}
\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\right)}
{\sqrt{\det\!\left(\mathbf{I}+\dfrac{t}{\sigma^2}
\tilde{A}\right)}}.
\label{eqn:mgf_Q_raw_case2}
\end{align}
Taking the logarithm of Eq.~\eqref{eqn:mgf_Q_raw_case2} and 
centering by $\mu_Q$ via Eq.~\eqref{eqn:mu_Q_case2}:
\begin{align}
\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{t(Q(\boldsymbol\beta)-\mu_Q)}\right] 
&= -\frac{1}{2}\log\det\!\left(\mathbf{I}+
\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\tilde{A}\right) - \frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A
\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A\right)^{-1}
\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y} \notag\\
&\;\;\;\;+ \frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\left(\mathrm{tr}(A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}) + 
\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\right).
\label{eqn:centered_mgf_Q_case2}
\end{align}
We bound each term in 
Eq.~\eqref{eqn:centered_mgf_Q_case2} separately.

\textit{Term 1 (log-determinant).} 
Applying the scalar inequality $-\log(1+x) \leq -x + 
x^2/(1-|x|)$ (valid for $|x|<1$) eigenvalue-by-eigenvalue 
to $\tilde{A}$, and using $\|t\tilde{A}/\sigma^2\|_{\mathrm{op}} 
\leq b_Q|t| < 1$:
\begin{align}
-\frac{1}{2}\log\det\!\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}
\tilde{A}\right) 
\leq -\frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\mathrm{tr}(\tilde{A}) + 
\frac{t^2\,\mathrm{tr}(\tilde{A}^2)}{2\sigma^4(1-b_Q|t|)},
\label{eqn:logdet_bound_case2}
\end{align}
where $\mathrm{tr}(\tilde{A}) = \mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A)$.

\textit{Term 2 (quadratic numerator).}
Since $(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A)^{-1}$ is 
not symmetric in general, we use operator norms directly. 
Note first that
\begin{align}
\left\|\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A\right\|_{\mathrm{op}} 
\leq \frac{|t|}{\sigma^2}\|\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq b_Q|t| < 1,
\label{eqn:op_norm_SigmaA}
\end{align}
so the resolvent bound gives
\begin{align}
\left\|\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A
\right)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq 
\frac{1}{1-b_Q|t|}.
\label{eqn:resolvent_bound}
\end{align}
Writing $(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A)^{-1} = 
\mathbf{I} - \frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A
(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A)^{-1}$ and 
applying Eq.~\eqref{eqn:resolvent_bound}:
\begin{align}
&-\frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A
\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A\right)^{-1}
\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y} \notag\\
=\;& -\frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y} 
+ \frac{t^2}{2\sigma^4}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A
\left(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A\right)^{-1}
\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y} \notag\\
\leq\;& -\frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y} + 
\frac{t^2\|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\|^2\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
\|\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}
{2\sigma^4(1-b_Q|t|)},
\label{eqn:numerator_bound_case2}
\end{align}
where the last step uses $\left|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A
(\mathbf{I}+\frac{t}{\sigma^2}\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A)^{-1}
\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\right| \leq \|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\|^2\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
\|\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathrm{op}}/(1-b_Q|t|)$ via 
Eq.~\eqref{eqn:resolvent_bound}.

\textit{Term 3 (centering).}
\begin{align}
\frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\left(\mathrm{tr}(A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}) + 
\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\right) 
= \frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\mathrm{tr}(\tilde{A}) + 
\frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}.
\label{eqn:centering_case2}
\end{align}

Combining Terms 1--3, the linear terms 
$-\frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\mathrm{tr}(\tilde{A})$ and 
$-\frac{t}{2\sigma^2}\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}^\top A\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}$ cancel 
exactly with Eq.~\eqref{eqn:centering_case2}, giving:
\begin{align}
\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{t(Q(\boldsymbol\beta)-\mu_Q)}\right] 
\leq \frac{t^2}{2(1-b_Q|t|)}\left(
\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\tilde{A}^2)}{\sigma^4} + 
\frac{\|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\|^2\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
\|\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\sigma^4}
\right) 
\triangleq \frac{t^2\nu^2_Q}{2(1-b_Q|t|)}.
\label{eqn:mgf_Q_bound_case2}
\end{align}
Bounding each term using $\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} \preceq \tau^2\mathbf{I}_d$, 
$\|A\|_F^2 \leq 8R^4$, $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq 2R^2$, and 
$\|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\| \leq n\tau^2M_yR/\sigma^2$:
\begin{align}
\mathrm{tr}(\tilde{A}^2) = \mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A\Sigma_\mathbf{Y} A) 
&\leq \tau^4\|A\|_F^2 \leq 8\tau^4R^4, 
\label{eqn:tr_final_case2}\\
\|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\|^2\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
\|\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\|_{\mathrm{op}} 
&\leq \frac{n^2\tau^4M_y^2R^2}{\sigma^4}
\cdot 4R^4 \cdot \tau^2 
= \frac{4n^2\tau^6M_y^2R^6}{\sigma^4},
\label{eqn:quad_final_case2}
\end{align}
so that
\begin{align}
\nu^2_Q \leq \frac{8\tau^4R^4}{\sigma^4} + 
\frac{4n^2\tau^6M_y^2R^6}{\sigma^8}.
\label{eqn:nu_Q_case2}
\end{align}

\textbf{Step 3: Combining via Cauchy-Schwarz.}
Since $L(\boldsymbol\beta)$ and $Q(\boldsymbol\beta)$ both depend on $\boldsymbol\beta$, 
they are not independent. Applying Cauchy-Schwarz:
\begin{align}
\log\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{t(c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)-\mu^{c_1}_\mathbf{Y})}
\right] 
&\leq \frac{1}{2}\log\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{2t(L-\mu_L)}
\right] + \frac{1}{2}\log\mathbb{E}\!\left[e^{2t(Q-\mu_Q)}
\right] \notag\\
&\leq \frac{(2t)^2\bar{\sigma}^2_L}{4} + 
\frac{(2t)^2\nu^2_Q}{4(1-b_Q|2t|)} \notag\\
&= t^2\bar{\sigma}^2_L + \frac{t^2\nu^2_Q}{1-2b_Q|t|} 
\notag\\
&\leq \frac{t^2(\bar{\sigma}^2_L+\nu^2_Q)}{1-2b_Q|t|} 
\triangleq \frac{t^2\nu^2_\mathbf{Y}/2}{1-b_\mathbf{Y}|t|},
\label{eqn:combined_case2}
\end{align}
where
\begin{align}
\nu^2_\mathbf{Y} &\triangleq 2(\bar{\sigma}^2_L+\nu^2_Q) \leq 
\frac{8M_y^2R^2\tau^2}{\sigma^4} + 
\frac{16\tau^4R^4}{\sigma^4} + 
\frac{8n^2\tau^6M_y^2R^6}{\sigma^8},
\label{eqn:nu_Y_case2}\\
b_\mathbf{Y} &\triangleq 2b_Q = \frac{4\tau^2R^2}{\sigma^2}.
\label{eqn:b_Y_case2}
\end{align}
This establishes that $c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ satisfies a 
sub-exponential MGF bound under $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y})$ with 
parameters $(\nu^2_\mathbf{Y}, b_\mathbf{Y})$ in the sense of 
Eq.~\eqref{eqn:subexp_con}.

For the reversed pair $(\mathbf{Y}',\mathbf{Y})$, $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=-c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=-L(\boldsymbol\beta)-Q(\boldsymbol\beta)$, i.e., $(L,Q,A)$ is replaced by $(-L,-Q,-A)$. Steps~1--3 use only $\Sigma\preceq\tau^2\mathbf I_d$, $\|\mathbf{x}_i\|\le R$, $|y_i|\le M_y$, $\|\boldsymbol\mu\|\le n\tau^2M_yR/\sigma^2$, $\|{-A}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ and $\|{-A}\|_F=\|A\|_F$, all of which also hold under $\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')$ since $\mathbf{Y}'$ also has $n$ records. Hence $\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)$ satisfies the same sub-exponential bound with parameters $(\nu^2_\mathbf{Y},b_\mathbf{Y})$ in Eqs.~\eqref{eqn:nu_Y_case2}--\eqref{eqn:b_Y_case2}.

\textbf{Step 4: Applying Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential}.}
Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential} requires $b_\mathbf{Y} < 
\min\{(\alpha-1)^{-1},1\}$. The condition $b_\mathbf{Y} < 1$ is an 
additional scale restriction requiring:
\begin{align}
\frac{4\tau^2R^2}{\sigma^2} < 1 
\iff \sigma^2 > 4\tau^2R^2.
\label{eqn:scale_condition}
\end{align}
Under Eq.~\eqref{eqn:scale_condition} and $\alpha < 1 + 
\sigma^2/(4\tau^2R^2)$, substituting $(\nu^2_\mathbf{Y}, b_\mathbf{Y})$ into 
Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential}:
\begin{align}
\max\{D_\alpha(P\|Q),D_\alpha(Q\|P)\} \leq \varepsilon_{\text{LR, Case2}}
(\alpha, b_\mathbf{Y}, \nu^2_\mathbf{Y}) \triangleq \frac{\nu^2_\mathbf{Y}}{2}\cdot
\frac{\alpha(1-2b_\mathbf{Y})+2b_\mathbf{Y}}
{(1-b_\mathbf{Y})\left(1-b_\mathbf{Y}(\alpha-1)\right)}.
\label{eqn:eps_LR_case2}
\end{align}

\begin{remark}
Three sources of looseness affect Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_LR_case2}. 
First, the Cauchy-Schwarz step doubles $b_Q$, restricting the 
valid range of $\alpha$. Second, the operator norm bound 
$\|\tilde{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq 2\tau^2R^2$ ignores 
data-dependent tightening of $\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}$ due to the likelihood. 
Third, the $n^2$-dependent term in $\nu^2_\mathbf{Y}$ arises from 
bounding $\|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\|$ using only row-wise boundedness; under 
a direct bound $\|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\| \leq C$ for some constant $C$, 
this term becomes $n$-independent. Despite this looseness, 
Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_LR_case2} yields an explicit finite-order 
RDP bound for OPS in the bounded-neighboring linear-regression 
setting with covariate replacement, and illustrates how the 
sub-exponential framework of Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential} extends 
beyond the sub-Gaussian cases treated in the main text.
\end{remark}

\bf\raggedright} 
{unbounded neighboring}
Let $\mathbf{Y}$ has size $n$ and $\mathbf{Y}'$ is of size $n-1$ by removing the $n$-th record $\mathbf{z}_n=(\mathbf{x}_n,y_n)$ in $\mathbf{Y}$. Then
\begin{align}
    c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=\log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y})}{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y}')}
    &=\log p(y_n|\mathbf{x}_n,\boldsymbol\beta)= -\frac{(y_n - \mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol{\beta})^2}{2\sigma^2} - \frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^2).\notag
\end{align}
This expression is structurally identical to Eq.~\eqref{eqn:GG_unbounded_c1} by setting $Z = y_n - \mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol{\beta}$, where 
\begin{align}
    Z|\mathbf{Y} \sim \mathcal{N}\left(y_n - \mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathbf{Y}, \mathbf{x}_n^\top \Sigma_\mathbf{Y} \mathbf{x}_n\right).\notag
\end{align}
In Proposition~\ref{thm:GG}, $\omega=\sigma^2/v_Y^2$, where $v_Y^2$ is the posterior variance of $\theta-y_n$; here the corresponding quantity is the posterior variance of $Z|\mathbf{Y}$. Thus, the corresponding $\omega_n$ here is $\omega_n =\frac{\sigma^2}{\mathbf{x}_n^\top \Sigma_\mathbf{Y} \mathbf{x}_n}$, plug in Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_GG_unbounded_ape}
\begin{align}
    D_\alpha(P\|Q) &= \underbrace{-\frac{\log(1-\omega_n^{-1})}{2} - \frac{\log(1+\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega_n})}{2(\alpha-1)}}_{A(\alpha, \omega_n)} + \frac{(\mathbf{x}_n^\top \boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathbf{Y} - y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2} \underbrace{\left[ \frac{1}{1-\omega_n^{-1}} - \frac{1}{1+\frac{\alpha-1}{\omega_n}} \right]}_{B(\alpha, \omega_n)}\notag\\
    & \le A(\alpha, \omega_n) + \frac{k^{*2}}{2}B(\alpha, \omega_n),
\end{align}
if $|\mathbf{x}_n^\top \boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathbf{Y} - y_n|\le k^*\sigma$ for some $k^*>0$.

\bf} 
{Reverse direction.}
We now bound $D_\alpha(\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}')\|\pi(\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}))$. The posterior under $\mathbf{Y}'$ is $\boldsymbol\beta|\mathbf{Y}'\sim\mathcal N(\boldsymbol\mu_{\mathbf{Y}'},\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'})$ with $\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}^{-1}=\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}^{-1}-\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{x}_n\mathbf{x}_n^\top$. Let $v_n'^2\triangleq\mathbf{x}_n^\top\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}\mathbf{x}_n$ and
\begin{align}
&\tilde c_1(\boldsymbol\beta)=\log\frac{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y}')}{\ell(\boldsymbol\beta;\mathbf{Y})}=\frac{(y_n-\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta)^2}{2\sigma^2}+\frac12\log(2\pi\sigma^2),\notag\\
&Z'\triangleq y_n-\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\beta\,|\,\mathbf{Y}'\sim\mathcal N(y_n-\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\mu_{\mathbf{Y}'},\,v_n'^2).
\end{align}
This is structurally identical to Eq.~\eqref{eqn:c1_GG_reverse} with $\mu_{Y'}$ replaced by $\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\mu_{\mathbf{Y}'}$ and $v_{Y'}^2$ replaced by $v_n'^2$. Hence, by Eq.~\eqref{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_v}, $D_\alpha(Q\|P)<\infty$ if and only if $(\alpha-1)v_n'^2<\sigma^2$. By the Sherman--Morrison formula,
\begin{align*}
&\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}=\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}-\frac{\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}\mathbf{x}_n\mathbf{x}_n^\top\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}}{\sigma^2+v_n'^2}\;\Rightarrow\;\mathbf{x}_n^\top\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{x}_n=\frac{\sigma^2v_n'^2}{\sigma^2+v_n'^2}\;\Rightarrow\;\omega_n=1+\frac{\sigma^2}{v_n'^2},\\ 
&\text{ i.e. }\ \frac{v_n'^2}{\sigma^2}=\frac{1}{\omega_n-1}.
\end{align*}
Moreover, $\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}=\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}^{-1}\boldsymbol\mu_{\mathbf{Y}'}+\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{x}_ny_n)$ and $\mathbf{x}_n^\top\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}^{-1}=\frac{\sigma^2}{\sigma^2+v_n'^2}\mathbf{x}_n^\top$, so
\begin{align}
\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}=\frac{\sigma^2}{\sigma^2+v_n'^2}\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\mu_{\mathbf{Y}'}+\frac{v_n'^2}{\sigma^2+v_n'^2}y_n\;\Rightarrow\;\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\mu_{\mathbf{Y}'}-y_n=\frac{\omega_n}{\omega_n-1}(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}-y_n).
\end{align}
These coincide with $v_{Y'}^2=\sigma^2/(\omega-1)$ and Eq.~\eqref{eqn:posterior_mean_reverse_relation} with $\omega$ replaced by $\omega_n$. Repeating the derivation of Eq.~\eqref{eqn:Dalpha_reverse_final}, when $1<\alpha<\omega_n$
\begin{align}
D_\alpha(Q\|P)=\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega_n)+\frac{(\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}-y_n)^2}{2\sigma^2}\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega_n)\le\widetilde A(\alpha,\omega_n)+\frac{k^{*2}}{2}\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega_n),
\end{align}
and $D_\alpha(Q\|P)=\infty$ for $\alpha\ge\omega_n$.

\bf} 
{Two-sided, data-independent guarantee.}
Since $\Sigma_{\mathbf{Y}'}\preceq\tau^2\mathbf I_d$ and $\|\mathbf{x}_n\|\le R$, $v_n'^2\le\tau^2R^2$, hence $\omega_n\ge\omega_{\min}\triangleq1+\sigma^2/(\tau^2R^2)$ for every admissible neighboring pair, with equality, e.g., when $n=1$ and $\|\mathbf{x}_1\|=R$. By the monotonicity of $A,B$ and $\widetilde A,\widetilde B$ in $\omega$ (Claim~\ref{claim:reverse-AB} and ~\ref{claim:mono}), for every $1<\alpha<\omega_{\min}$,
\begin{align}
&\max\{D_\alpha(P\|Q),D_\alpha(Q\|P)\}\notag\\
\le\;&\max\Big\{A(\alpha,\omega_{\min})+\frac{k^{*2}}{2}B(\alpha,\omega_{\min}),\ \widetilde A(\alpha,\omega_{\min})+\frac{k^{*2}}{2}\widetilde B(\alpha,\omega_{\min})\Big\},
\end{align}
whereas for $\alpha\ge\omega_{\min}$ there exist admissible neighboring pairs with $D_\alpha(Q\|P)=\infty$. In particular, OPS does not satisfy pure DP in this setting.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
Three sources of looseness affect Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_LR_case2}. 
First, the Cauchy-Schwarz step doubles $b_Q$, restricting the 
valid range of $\alpha$. Second, the operator norm bound 
$\|\tilde{A}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq 2\tau^2R^2$ ignores 
data-dependent tightening of $\Sigma_\mathbf{Y}$ due to the likelihood. 
Third, the $n^2$-dependent term in $\nu^2_\mathbf{Y}$ arises from 
bounding $\|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\|$ using only row-wise boundedness; under 
a direct bound $\|\boldsymbol\mu_\mathbf{Y}\| \leq C$ for some constant $C$, 
this term becomes $n$-independent. Despite this looseness, 
Eq.~\eqref{eqn:eps_LR_case2} yields an explicit finite-order 
RDP bound for OPS in the bounded-neighboring linear-regression 
setting with covariate replacement, and illustrates how the 
sub-exponential framework of Corollary~\ref{coro:subexponential} extends 
beyond the sub-Gaussian cases treated in the main text.
<!-- end remark-5 -->

        \item The answer \textcolor{gray}{[N/A]}{} means that the abstract and introduction do not include the claims made in the paper.

A \textcolor{orange}{[No]}{} or \textcolor{gray}{[N/A]}{} answer to this question will not be perceived well by the reviewers. 
        \item The claims made should match theoretical and experimental results, and reflect how much the results can be expected to generalize to other settings. 
        \item It is fine to include aspirational goals as motivation as long as it is clear that these goals are not attained by the paper.
